X^{\prime}
, so there is an
(I_{1}\cup\cdots\cup I_{j-1})
-
X^{\prime}
linkage
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
of order
|X^{\prime}|
in
G_{1}\cup\cdots\cup G_{j-1}
. Symmetrically, there is an
X^{\prime\prime}
–
(I_{j+1}\cup\cdots\cup I_{k})
-linkage
\mathcal{P}^{\prime\prime}
of order
|X^{\prime\prime}|
in
G_{j+1}\cup\cdots\cup G_{k}
.
Apply Menger’s Theorem to
G_{j}
and the sets
X^{\prime}\cup V(I^{\prime})
and
X^{\prime\prime}\cup V(I^{\prime\prime})
. If we get a linkage of order
p
, then we can use the paths from
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{P}^{\prime\prime}
to extend the paths with endpoints in
X^{\prime}
or
X^{\prime\prime}
so that they have endpoints in
v_{1}\cdots v_{i_{1}-1}
or
v_{i_{2}+1}\cdots v_{n}
respectively, and we obtain a desired transaction of order
p
.
Otherwise, we get an
(X^{\prime}\cup V(I^{\prime}))
-
(X^{\prime\prime}\cup V(I^{\prime\prime}))
-separation
(G^{\prime},G^{\prime\prime})
of
G_{j}
with order less than
p
, and an
(X^{\prime}\cup V(I^{\prime}))
-
(X^{\prime\prime}\cup V(I^{\prime\prime}))
-linkage of the same order. As before, we can extend it to a
\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{i}\}
–
\{v_{i+1},\ldots,v_{i}\}
linkage, so the invariant is satisfied, and we can safely replace
I_{j}
with
I^{\prime}
and
I^{\prime\prime}
, and
G_{j}
with
G^{\prime}
and
G^{\prime\prime}
.

Let us analyse the time complexity of the described algorithm.
Consider the sequence
(G_{1},\ldots,G_{k})
at any moment of the execution.
We refer to the graphs
G_{j}
as the
parts
of
G
.
Observe that if the sequence of parts was
(G_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,G_{k^{\prime}}^{\prime})
at an earlier stage of the execution, then each new part
G_{j}
is a subgraph of some old part
G_{j^{\prime}}^{\prime}
. After an execution of Menger’s Theorem, the part
G^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}}
splits into two parts whose corresponding segments are roughly two times smaller length, and
G_{j}
is contained in one of these parts.
Hence, during the whole execution of the algorithm, Menger’s Theorem is applied to a part containing
G_{j}
only
\mathbf{O}(\log|V(G)|)
times. Since the time complexity of running Menger’s Theorem on a part
G_{j^{\prime}}^{\prime}
is
\mathbf{O}(p|E(G_{j^{\prime}}^{\prime})|)
, the total time complexity is
\mathbf{O}(p|E(G)|\log|V(G)|)
.
∎

The following is an easy consequence of this lemma and the fact that a society with a linear decomposition of adhesion at most
p
has depth at most
2p
, which we already noted in
Section

3.4
.

Corollary 9.7
.

Let
p
be a positive integer and let
(G,\Omega)
be a society with depth at least
2p
.
Then we can find a transaction of order
p
in
\mathbf{O}(p|E(G)|\log|V(G)|)
-time.

9.4
Constructively finding crooked transactions

The proof of
Proposition

9.3
that Kawarabayashi et al. present in
[
KTW21
]
can be split into three major components.
To describe them, we first need a technical definition that describes a planar, flat, isolated, separating transaction in reference to which one “side” of the society is flat.

Definition 9.8

(Transactions that are flat on one side)
.

Let
p
be an integer with
p\geq 2
, let
(G,\Omega)
be a society, and let
\mathcal{P}=\{P_{1},\ldots,P_{p}\}
be a planar, flat, isolated, separating transaction in
(G,\Omega)
that is indexed naturally, with
\rho
being a vortex-free rendition of the
\mathcal{P}
-strip society of
(G,\Omega)
.
We let
I
, respectively
I^{\prime}
, be the unique segment of
\Omega
that has the same endpoints as
P_{p-1}
, respectively
P_{2}
, and does not contain the endpoints of
P_{p}
, respectively
P_{1}
.

Let
H_{1}
be the graph consisting of
\mathcal{P}\setminus\{P_{p}\}
and the vertices in
I
, and let
G_{1}
be the graph consisting of the union of
H_{1}
and all
H_{1}
-bridges with at least one attachment in
V(H_{1})\setminus V(P_{p-1})
.
32
32

32

Due to
\mathcal{P}
being flat and separating, no such bridge can contain a vertex of
V(P_{p})
or
V(\Omega)\setminus I
.

The society
(G_{1},\Omega_{1})
is defined by choosing
\Omega_{1}
such that
V(\Omega_{1})=I\cup(N(\rho)\cap V(P_{p-1}))
, with the order on
I
being obtained naturally from
\Omega
and the order on
N(\rho)\cap V(P_{p-1})
being derived from a traversal of
P_{p-1}
.
We define
H_{p}
,
G_{p}
, and
\Omega_{p}
analogously based on the
I^{\prime}
and the paths
\mathcal{P}\setminus\{P_{1}\}
, with
P_{2}
playing the role of
P_{p-1}
.

We say that
\mathcal{P}
is
flat on one side
if
(G_{1},\Omega_{1})
or
(G_{p},\Omega_{p})
has a vortex-free rendition in a disk.

The three components of the proof of
Proposition

9.3
in
[
KTW21
]
can now be described as follows:

i)

Find a large flat, planar transaction that is flat on one side in the absence of a large crooked transaction.

ii)

Iteratively build a well-structured cylindrical rendition whenever we fail to find a large crooked transaction.

iii)

Reroute a crooked transaction onto the original society using the well-structured cylindrical rendition built in the second step.

Both the second and third point are taken care of by using minimality arguments in
[
KTW21
]
.
We will demonstrate constructive procedures with explicit runtimes for both of these steps.

Despite the somewhat complicated definition the first component entails, the statements directly associated with it are simple to prove.
We will directly follow the arguments presented in
[
KTW21
]
.
The bounds for our version of
Proposition

9.3
will be slightly worse, since splitting the proof of
Proposition

9.3
into several statements prevents us from reusing certain renditions with convenient properties.
Our first lemma shows that any large planar transaction yields a large crooked transaction or a large planar, flat transaction that is flat on one side.
The specificity in the first point of the statement will be useful later on in
Section

11
.

Proposition 9.9

(Kawarabayashi, Thomas, and Wollan
[
KTW21
]
)
.

Let
p,q
be positive integers with
p,q\geq 2
, let
(G,\Omega)
be a society, and let
\mathcal{P}
be a planar transaction in
(G,\Omega)
of order
3q+2p-3
.
Then in
(G,\Omega)
there exists

i)

a crooked transaction
\mathcal{Q}
of order
q
such that

•

\mathcal{Q}
is a leap with the overpass
Q
and
\mathcal{Q}\setminus\{Q\}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
, or

•

\mathcal{Q}
is a doublecross containing two crosses
P,P^{\prime}
and
R,R^{\prime}
, and
\mathcal{Q}\setminus\{P,P^{\prime},R,R^{\prime}\}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
, or

ii)

a planar, flat, isolated, separating transaction
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
of order
p
that is flat on one side.

Furthermore, we can find one of these two transactions in time
\mathbf{O}(pq|E(G)|)
.

Proof.

Let
p^{\prime}\coloneqq 3q+2p-3
and let
\mathcal{P}=\{P_{1},\ldots,P_{p^{\prime}}\}
be indexed naturally.
Let
A,B
be the end segments of
\mathcal{P}
, let
a_{i}\in A
be an endpoint of
P_{i}
, and let
b_{i}\in B
be an endpoint of
P_{i}
for each
i\in[p^{\prime}]
.

We let
H
be the subgraph of
G
obtained from the union of the elements of
\mathcal{P}
and
V(\Omega)
.
Further, let
G_{1}^{\prime}
be the subgraph of
H
consisting of
\bigcup_{i=1}^{q+p-1}P_{i}
and all vertices in the segment
S_{1}
of
\Omega
that ends on
a_{q+p-1}
and
b_{q+p-1}
and contains the endpoints of
P_{1}
.
Consider all
H
-bridges with at least one attachment in
V(G_{1}^{\prime})\setminus V(P_{q+p-1})
, and for each such bridge
B
, let
B^{\prime}
denote the graph obtained from
B
by deleting all attachments that are not contained in
V(G_{1}^{\prime})
.
Finally, let
G_{1}
denote the union of
G_{1}^{\prime}
and all graphs
B^{\prime}
as above.
The cyclic permutation
\Omega_{1}
is then defined by saying that
V(\Omega_{1})=S_{1}
, with the order of
\Omega_{1}
being obtained naturally from
\Omega
.
Thus
(G_{1},\Omega_{1})
is a society and we define the society
(G_{2},\Omega_{2})
analogously using the paths
P_{2q+p-1},\ldots,P_{p^{\prime}}
instead, with
P_{2q+p-1}
playing the role of
P_{q+p-1}
.

If
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
are not disjoint then
G_{1}\cup G_{2}
includes a path
P
from
S_{1}
to the segment of
\Omega
with the endpoints
a_{2q+p-1},b_{2q+p-1}
that contains the endpoints of
P_{p^{\prime}}
.
The path
P
must be disjoint from
P_{q+p},\ldots,P_{2q+p-2}
, which means that
P
together with these paths forms a crooked transaction of order
q
, as we desire.
Thus we can assume that
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
are disjoint.

Should both societies
(G_{1},\Omega_{1})
and
(G_{2},\Omega_{2})
contain a cross, then the union of the two crosses and
P_{q+p},\ldots,P_{2q+p-2}
form a crooked transaction of order more than
q
, again satisfying the first item of our statement.
We can therefore assume without loss of generality that
(G_{1},\Omega_{1})
has a vortex-free rendition
\rho_{1}^{\prime}
in a disk
\Delta_{1}^{\prime}
.

For each
i\in[p]
we let
T_{i}
be the trace of
P_{i}
in
\rho_{1}^{\prime}
and let
D_{i}
be the disk in
\Delta_{1}^{\prime}\setminus T_{i}
that does not contain
\pi^{-1}(a_{p+1})
.
We let
J_{i}
be the crop of
G_{1}
to
D_{i}
.
Since
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
is indexed naturally, we have
J_{i}\subseteq J_{j}
whenever
i,j\in[p]
and
i<j
.

Suppose that there exists some
i\in[p]
and an edge in
G
with one endpoint
u
in
J_{i}-\pi(N(\rho_{1}^{\prime})\cap T_{i})
and the other endpoint
v
in
G-V(J_{i})
.
Due to
T_{i}
being a trace in
\rho_{1}^{\prime}
, we conclude that
v\not\in V(G_{1})
.
Thus there exists a path
Q_{1}
in
J_{i}
connecting
u
and a vertex in
V(\Omega)
and a path
Q_{2}
connecting
v
and a vertex of
V(\Omega)
in
G-V(G_{1})
.
Since
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
must be disjoint,
Q_{1}\cup Q_{2}
together with
uv
make up a path that forms a crooked transaction of order
q
together with the paths
P_{p+1},\ldots,P_{q+p-1}
.
Thus, we may suppose that this is not the case for any
i\in[p]
.

Let
\mathcal{Q}=\{P_{1},\ldots,P_{p}\}
.
Using
\rho_{1}^{\prime}
, we can construct a vortex-free rendition
\rho^{\prime\prime}
of the
\mathcal{Q}
-strip society of
(G,\Omega)
that is a restriction of
\rho_{1}^{\prime}
.
In particular, our arguments imply that
\mathcal{Q}
is a planar, flat, isolated, separating transaction of order
p
in
(G,\Omega)
.
Furthermore,
\mathcal{Q}
is flat on one side, deriving from the fact that
\rho_{1}^{\prime}
already yields a vortex-free rendition of
(G_{1},\Omega_{1})
and our above observations on the graphs
J_{1},\ldots,J_{p}
.
This concludes our proof.
∎

This allows us to show that in a society with large depth we can find a large crooked transaction or a large flat transaction that is flat on one side.
We could give a
6q+2
bound on the depth of the society if we were to apply
Lemma

9.5
, which is slightly worse than the
6q
bound given in
[
KTW21
]
.
However, we will be using
Corollary

9.7
and thus incur another factor 2 in this bound.

Proposition 9.10

(Kawarabayashi, Thomas, and Wollan
[
KTW21
]
)
.

Let
q
be an integer with
q\geq 2
and let
(G,\Omega)
be a society with depth at least
12q+4
.
Then in
(G,\Omega)
there exists

i)

a crooked transaction of order
q
, or

ii)

a planar, flat, isolated, separating transaction of order
q+4
that is flat on one side.

Furthermore, we can find one of these two transactions in time
\mathcal{O}(q|E(G)|(q+\log|V(G)|))
.

Proof.

Since
(G,\Omega)
has depth at least
12q+4
, we can find a transaction
\mathcal{P}
in
(G,\Omega)
of order
6q+2
using
Corollary

9.7
.
Furthermore, using
Proposition

9.2
, we can either find a crooked transaction of order
q
within
\mathcal{P}
, which would mean we are done, or there exists a planar transaction
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
of order
5q+5
.
Applying
Proposition

9.9
to
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
now yields one of the desired outcomes.
∎

The second component of the proof of
Proposition

9.3
in
[
KTW21
]
requires us to introduce a new data structure to track the iterative progress we make towards embedding the society.

Definition 9.11

(Constriction of a society)
.

Let
q,\ell
be non-negative integers with
q\geq 3
, let
(G_{0},\Omega_{0})
be a society, and let
\rho_{0}
be the cylindrical rendition in a disk
\Delta_{0}
with
\Delta_{0}
its unique vortex and cell.
We call
\mathfrak{N}=(G_{0},\Omega_{0},\mathfrak{P})
a
(q,\ell)
-constriction of
(G_{0},\Omega_{0})
if

C1

the set
\mathfrak{P}=\{\mathcal{P}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{P}_{\ell}\}
is a set of linkages in
G_{0}
such that
\mathcal{P}_{i}=\{P_{1}^{i},\ldots,P_{q}^{i}\}
for each
i\in[\ell]
,

C2

there exist societies
(G_{1},\Omega_{1}),\ldots,(G_{\ell},\Omega_{\ell})
such that for each
i\in[\ell]
, we have
G_{i}\subseteq G_{i-1}-V(P_{1}^{i})
and
\mathcal{P}_{i}
is a planar, flat, isolated, separating transaction in
(G_{i-1},\Omega_{i-1})
that is indexed naturally and flat on one side,
33
33

33

In the context of this definition the flat side will be the one that contains
P_{1}^{i}
.

C3

for all
i\in[\ell]
, there exists a cylindrical rendition
\rho_{i}
of
(G_{i},\Omega_{i})
in a
\rho_{i-1}
-aligned disk
\Delta_{i}\subseteq\Delta_{i-1}
around the vortex
c_{i}
, with
\{P_{3}^{i},\ldots,P_{q-1}^{i}\}
being an unexposed transaction in
(G_{i},\Omega_{i})
in
\rho_{i}
, and

C4

for all
i\in[\ell]
, the
\Delta_{i}
-society in
\rho_{i-1}
is
(G_{i},\Omega_{i})
, we have
V(\Omega_{i})\subseteq V(\Omega_{i-1})\cup V(P_{2}^{i})
, and
\widetilde{c}_{i}\subseteq\widetilde{c}_{i-1}\cup V(P_{q}^{i})
.

See
figure

41
for an illustration.
The society
(G_{\ell},\Omega_{\ell})
is called the
frontier of
\mathfrak{N}
, with the
frontier-rendition
\rho_{\ell}
in the disk
\Delta_{\ell}
.
In particular, according to our definition there exists a cylindrical rendition
\rho
of
(G_{0},\Omega_{0})
in
\Delta_{0}
around
c_{i}
such that
\rho_{\ell}
is a restriction of
\rho
to
\Delta_{\ell}
.
We call
\rho
the
total rendition for
\mathfrak{N}
.
Since we allow for
\ell=0
, each society has a trivial
(q,0)
-constriction.

c_{3}
P^{1}_{1}
P^{2}_{1}
P^{3}_{1}
(G_{3},\Omega_{3})

Figure 41
:
A
(6,3)
-constriction of a society
(G,\Omega)
. Each of the three linkages
\mathcal{P}_{1}
(depicted in
blue
),
\mathcal{P}_{2}
(depicted in
yellow
), and
\mathcal{P}_{3}
(depicted in
green
) is a transaction on its corresponding restricted society that is flat on one side (indicated by the coloured areas).
The dashed line indicates the society
(G_{3},\Omega_{3})
.
Notice that
(G_{3},\Omega_{3})
has a cylindrical rendition where the coloured parts are all disjoint from the unique vortex
c_{3}
.

We now show that we can always find a crooked transaction in the frontier, verify that the entire society has a cylindrical rendition of bounded depth, or we can extend a given constriction.

Lemma 9.12
.

Let
q,\ell
be non-negative integers with
2\leq q
.
Let
(G,\Omega)
be a society with a
(q+3,\ell)
-constriction
\mathfrak{N}=(G,\Omega,\mathfrak{P})
with the frontier
(H,\Omega^{\prime})
.
Then either

i)

there exists a crooked transaction of order at least
q
in
(H,\Omega^{\prime})
,

ii)

there exists a cylindrical rendition of
(G,\Omega)
of depth at most
12q+4
, or

iii)

there exists a transaction
\mathcal{Q}
in
(H,\Omega^{\prime})
such that
(G,\Omega,\mathfrak{P}\cup\{\mathcal{Q}\})
is a
(q+3,\ell+1)
-constriction.

Moreover, there exists an algorithm that leads to one of these options in time
\mathbf{O}(q|E(G)|\log|V(G)|)
.

Proof.

Let
\rho=(\Gamma,\mathcal{D})
be the total rendition for
\mathfrak{N}
in a disk
\Delta
and let
\rho_{H}
be the frontier rendition of
(H,\Omega^{\prime})
in a disk
\Delta_{H}\subseteq\Delta
.
If
(H,\Omega^{\prime})
has depth at most
12q+4
, then
(\Gamma,(\mathcal{D}\cup\{\Delta_{H}\})\setminus\{c\in\mathcal{D}~\!\colon\!~c\subseteq\Delta_{H}\})
is a cylindrical rendition of
(G,\Omega)
of depth at most
12q+4
.

Thus we may suppose that
(H,\Omega^{\prime})
has depth at least
12q+4
.
By applying
Proposition

9.10
to
(H,\Omega^{\prime})
we therefore either find a crooked transaction of order
q
in
(H,\Omega^{\prime})
, or we find a planar, flat, isolated separating transaction
\mathcal{P}=\{P_{1},\ldots,P_{q+4}\}
of order
q+4
in
(H,\Omega^{\prime})
that is flat on one side.
Since the first option is one of our goals, we may suppose that we find
\mathcal{P}
, which we choose to be indexed naturally such that
P_{1}
is contained on the side of
\mathcal{P}
that has a vortex-free rendition.

Let
I
be the segment of
\Omega
with the same endpoints as
P_{q+4}
that contains all other endpoints of the paths in
\mathcal{P}

Further, let
H^{*}
be the subgraph of
G
consisting of the paths in
\mathcal{P}
and the vertices in
I
.
We let
G^{\prime}
be the union of
H^{*}
and all
H^{*}
-bridges with at least one attachment in
H^{*}-V(P_{q+4})
and let the cyclic ordering
\Omega^{\prime\prime}
of
V(\Omega^{\prime\prime})=I
be defined naturally via the order of
\Omega
on
I
.
Since
\mathcal{P}
is flat, isolated, separating, and flat on the side that contains
P_{1}
, the society
(G^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime\prime})
has a vortex-free rendition
\rho^{\prime}
in a disk
\Delta^{\prime}\subseteq\Delta_{H}
.

We let
T_{q+3}
be the trace of
P_{q+3}
in
\rho^{\prime}
and let
\Delta_{1}
be the closure of the disk in
\Delta^{\prime}-T_{q+3}
that contains the trace of
P_{1}
, with
G_{1}^{\prime}
being the crop of
G^{\prime}
to
\Delta_{1}
.
Let
I^{\prime}
be the unique segment of
\Omega^{\prime\prime}
that has the same endpoints as
P_{q+3}
and contains both endpoints of
P_{q+4}
.
This allows us to define a cylindrical rendition
\rho^{\prime\prime}
of
(H,\Omega^{\prime})
as follows.
We embed
G_{1}^{\prime}
as in
\rho^{\prime}
– which does not involve any vortices, as
\rho^{\prime}
is vortex-free – and introduce a single vortex
c^{\prime}
that contains the entirety of
H[(V(H)\setminus V(G_{1}))\cup(N(\rho^{\prime})\cap V(P_{q+3}))]-E(G_{1}^{\prime})
drawn onto
c^{\prime}
such that exactly the vertices in
N(\rho^{\prime})\cap V(P_{q+3})
and
I^{\prime}
appear on its boundary in a manner fitting
\Omega^{\prime\prime}
and the order of vertices on
P_{q+3}
.
Within
\rho^{\prime\prime}
, we let
T_{2}
be the trace of
P_{2}
and let
\Delta_{\ell+1}
be the closure of the disk in
\Delta_{H}-T_{2}
that contains the trace of
P_{3}
, with
(H^{\prime},\Psi)
being the
\Delta_{\ell+1}
-society in
\rho^{\prime\prime}
.

The cylindrical rendition
\rho^{\prime\prime}
, the society
(H^{\prime},\Psi)
, and the transaction
\mathcal{Q}=\{P_{1},\ldots,P_{q+3}\}
in
(H,\Omega^{\prime})
together confirm that
(G,\Omega,\mathfrak{P}\cup\{\mathcal{Q}\})
is a
(q+3,\ell+1)
-constriction, which completes our proof.
∎

A simple induction using repeated applications of the above lemma yields the following corollary.
The worsening of the runtime as compared to
Lemma

9.12
is due to our inability to bound the number of times we have to refine the constriction.

Corollary 9.13
.

Let
q,\ell
be non-negative integers with
2\leq q
and let
(G,\Omega)
be a society.
Then

i)

there exists a cylindrical rendition
\rho
of
(G,\Omega)
of depth at most
12q+4
, or

ii)

there exists a
(q+3,\ell)
-constriction
\mathfrak{N}
of
(G,\Omega)
with the frontier
(H,\Omega^{\prime})
and a crooked transaction
\mathcal{Q}
of order at least
q
in
(H,\Omega^{\prime})
.

Moreover, there exists an algorithm that finds
\rho
or
\mathfrak{N}
and
\mathcal{Q}
in time
\mathbf{O}(q|E(G)||V(G)|\log|V(G)|)
.

The first option of this corollary is of course something we would welcome in the context of
Proposition

9.3
.
Thus our last task will be move the orthogonal transaction we find in the frontier of the constriction back onto the original society.
For this purpose Kawarabayashi et al. use a pair of so-called exchange lemmas for crooked transactions from
[
RS90b
]
.
We will ultimately only need one of the two, as the first – Lemma 3.4 in
[
KTW21
]
and (3.2) in
[
RS90b
]
– is only used to prove the second lemma.
The lemma that we will need is Lemma 3.5 in
[
KTW21
]
and (3.3) in
[
RS90b
]
.
As we do not need the techniques contained in its proof, we only give its statement.

Proposition 9.14

(Robertson and Seymour
[
RS90b
]
)
.

Let
(G,\Omega)
be a society and let
\mathcal{P}
be a crooked transaction in
(G,\Omega)
.
Let
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
be disjoint paths, each with one endpoint in
V(\Omega)
, the other endpoint in
V(\mathcal{P})
, and no other vertex in
V(\mathcal{P})
.
There exists a path
P\in\mathcal{P}
and subpaths
R_{1},R_{2}
of
P
such that one of the following is a crooked transaction:

i)

(\mathcal{P}\setminus\{P\})\cup\{R_{1}\cup Q_{1}\}
,

ii)

(\mathcal{P}\setminus\{P\})\cup\{R_{2}\cup Q_{2}\}
, or

iii)

(\mathcal{P}\setminus\{P\})\cup\{R\cup Q_{1},R_{2}\cup Q_{2}\}
.

We will also need the following simple lemma about shrinking crooked transactions.

Proposition 9.15

(Kawarabayashi, Thomas, and Wollan
[
KTW21
]
)
.

Let
(G,\Omega)
be a society and let
\mathcal{P}
be a crooked transaction of order at least five in
(G,\Omega)
.
Then there exists a path
P\in\mathcal{P}
such that
\mathcal{P}\setminus\{P\}
is a crooked transaction.

We are now ready to prove the last of the major steps mentioned earlier.
The arguments presented here are essentially taken directly from the proof of
Proposition

9.3
in
[
KTW21
]
, though we added some observations on how the arguments affect the runtime of the process.

Lemma 9.16
.

Let
q,\ell
be integers with
q\geq 4
and
\ell\geq 1
.
Let
(G,\Omega)
be a society with a
(q+3,\ell)
-constriction
\mathfrak{N}=(G,\Omega,\mathfrak{P}=\{\mathcal{P}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{P}_{\ell}\})
and a crooked transaction of order
q
in the frontier
(H,\Psi)
of
\mathfrak{N}
.

Then there exists a crooked transaction
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
of order
q
in the frontier
(H^{\prime},\Psi^{\prime})
of the
(q+3,\ell-1)
-constriction
\mathfrak{N}^{\prime}=(G,\Omega,\mathfrak{P}\setminus\{\mathcal{P}_{\ell}\})
such that the endpoints of
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
are found in
V(\Psi)\cap V(\Psi^{\prime})
.

In particular, there exists an algorithm that finds
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
in time
\mathbf{O}(q|E(G)||V(G)|)
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{Q}
be the crooked transaction of order
q
in
(H,\Psi)
.
Furthermore, we let
\mathcal{P}_{\ell}=\{P_{1},\ldots,P_{q+3}\}
be indexed naturally such that
P_{1}\not\subseteq H
.
We also let
\rho
be the frontier rendition of
(H,\Psi)
around the vortex
c
in a disk
\Delta
.

Recall that according to
Definition

9.11
the paths
P_{3},P_{4},\ldots,P_{q+2}
are unexposed in
\rho
, vertices of
P_{2}
form part of the set
V(\Psi)
, and vertices of
P_{q+3}
form part of the boundary of
c
.
Accordingly, we have
V(\Psi)\setminus V(\Psi^{\prime})\subseteq V(P_{2})
and in particular, the endpoints of
P_{2}
are both found in
V(\Psi)
.
Let
I
be the segment of
\Psi
that has the same endpoints as
P_{2}
and only contains vertices also found in
V(P_{2})
.
If all endpoints of
\mathcal{Q}
are found outside of
I
, there is nothing to prove.
Thus we may assume from this point forward, that some path in
\mathcal{Q}
has an endpoint in
I
.

Our goal from now on will be to iteratively minimise the size of
|E(\mathcal{Q})\cup E(\{P_{3},P_{4},\ldots,P_{q+3}\}|
, which is originally bounded by
|E(H)|\leq|E(G)|
, and show that any such linkage cannot have any endpoints in
I
.
For later use, let
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}=\{P_{3},P_{4},\ldots,P_{q+3}\}
.

Towards the goal of minimising the size of the set defined above, we will assume that the set is already of minimal size and point out how costly the construction of an improvement – which will generally be achieved by finding a new crooked transaction that shares more edges with the paths in
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
– would be whenever we encounter a contradiction to the minimality of this quantity.

Let us first confirm the following claim.

Claim 9.16.1
.

No path in
\mathcal{Q}
is unexposed in
\rho
.

Proof of
Claim

9.16.1
:

As
\rho
is a cylindrical rendition with the vortex
c
, any such path would in particular be grounded and thus have a trace
T
.
This defines two disks within
\Delta-T
, one of which is disjoint from
c
.
Let this disk be
d
.
We can choose our unexposed path within
\mathcal{Q}
to minimise
d
(by containment within the other disks).
This path must be a peripheral path of
\mathcal{Q}
, contradicting the fact that
\mathcal{Q}
is crooked.

\blacksquare

This allows us to now easily deduce the following useful property.

Claim 9.16.2
.

If a path
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
has an endpoint in
I
, then it intersects each path within
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
.
Furthermore, for each
i\in[3,q+3]
, at least two distinct paths in
\mathcal{Q}
intersect
P_{i}
.

Proof of
Claim

9.16.2
:

The first part of the claim is a consequence of
Claim

9.16.1
, as the endpoint of
Q
in
I
and
\rho
being a cylindrical rendition around
c
otherwise forces
Q
to be unexposed, leading to a contradiction.

Towards the second part of the claim, choose some
i\in[3,q+3]
such that
P_{i}
is only intersected by a unique path
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
.
This
P_{i}
must exist due to the first part of our claim and our assumption that some path in
\mathcal{Q}
has an endpoint
u
in
I
.
Let
R
be a subpath of
P_{i}
with one endpoint
v
in
V(\Psi)
and the other
w
in
V(Q)
such that
R
is internally disjoint from
Q
.
Since
P_{i}
is disjoint from all paths in
\mathcal{Q}\setminus\{Q\}
and the path
uQwP_{i}v
is unexposed,
Claim

9.16.1
implies that no path in
\mathcal{Q}
has an endpoint in the segment
I^{\prime}
of
\Psi
that ends on
u
and
v
but does not contain the other endpoint
u^{\prime}
of
Q
.
Therefore
(\mathcal{Q}\setminus\{Q\})\cup\{u^{\prime}QwP_{i}v\}
is a crooked transaction in
(H,\Psi)
, which we could identify and construct in time
\mathbf{O}(q|E(G)|)
.
Since at least one edge of
uQw
is not contained in this new transaction, this contradicts the minimality condition on
\mathcal{Q}
, proving our claim.

\blacksquare

As a consequence of
Claim

9.16.2
, we now know that
V(\mathcal{Q})\cap V(\mathcal{P}^{\prime})\neq\emptyset
.

For our next arguments, we call an endpoint
x
of
P_{i}\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}

available
if no path in
\mathcal{Q}
has
x
as an endpoint.
Since
\mathcal{Q}
has order
q
, there exist at least two available vertices.
Let
x
and
y
be two such available vertices, where
x\in V(P_{i})
and
y\in V(P_{j})
for
i,j\in[3,q+3]
(possibly with
i=j
).
Further, let
R_{x}
be a minimal subpath of
P_{i}
ending in
x
and a vertex of
V(\mathcal{Q})
and let
R_{y}\subseteq P_{j}
be defined analogously.

Suppose
R_{x}
and
R_{y}
intersect.
Then
i=j
must hold and in particular
R_{x}
and
R_{y}
intersect in a single vertex.
This however implies that a unique path from
\mathcal{Q}
is intersecting
P_{i}
, which contradicts
Claim

9.16.2
.
Thus
R_{x}
and
R_{y}
must be disjoint.

We apply
Proposition

9.14
to
\mathcal{Q}
,
R_{x}
, and
R_{y}
, which tells us that there exists a
Q^{\prime}
and two subpaths
Q_{1},Q_{2}\subseteq Q^{\prime}
such that one of the three outcomes of
Proposition

9.14
holds for
Q^{\prime}
,
Q_{1}
,
Q_{2}
,
R_{x}
, and
R_{y}
.
Note that, given
\mathcal{Q}
,
R_{x}
, and
R_{y}
, we can determine which of the three outcomes of
Proposition

9.14
holds in time
\mathbf{O}(q|E(G)|)
.

Suppose that one of the first two outcomes holds and suppose further w.l.o.g. that
(\mathcal{Q}\cup\{Q^{\prime}\})\cup\{R_{x}\cup Q_{1}\}
is a crooked transaction.
Due to the minimality assumptions on
\mathcal{Q}
, our new crooked transaction must contain the same edges as
\mathcal{Q}
.
Thus, every edge of the path
Q^{\prime}-Q_{1}
is contained in
E(\bigcup_{P\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}P)
.
As a consequence
V(P_{i})=V(R_{x})\cup V(Q^{\prime}-Q_{1})
, which implies that
Q^{\prime}
is the unique path in
\mathcal{Q}
that intersects
P_{i}
, contradicting
Claim

9.16.2
.

We may therefore move on to the third option of
Proposition

9.14
.
Let
Q_{3}
be the minimal subpath of
Q^{\prime}
with its endpoints in
V(R_{x})
and
V(R_{y})
such that
Q^{\prime}=Q_{1}\cup Q_{2}\cup Q_{3}
.
If
Q_{3}
has at least one edge outside of
E(\bigcup_{P\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}P)
then our new crooked transaction contradicts the minimality of
\mathcal{Q}
, thanks to
Proposition

9.15
.

This means that
E(Q_{3})
is a proper subset of
E(\bigcup_{P\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}P)
.
Since
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
is a linkage,
Q_{3}
must therefore be contained in a single path found in
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
and this can only be
P_{i}
with
i=j
.
This however again implies that
Q^{\prime}
is the only path intersecting
P_{i}
, contradicting
Claim

9.16.2
once more and completing our proof.
∎

We can employ induction together with this new lemma to derive a very useful corollary.

Corollary 9.17
.

Let
q,\ell
be positive integers with
4\leq q
.
Let
(G,\Omega)
be a society with a
(q+3,\ell)
-constriction
\mathfrak{N}
and a crooked transaction of order
q
in the frontier of
\mathfrak{N}
.

Then there exists a crooked transaction of order
q
in
(G,\Omega)
.

In particular, there exists an algorithm that finds this crooked transaction in time
\mathbf{O}(q|E(G)||V(G)|^{2})
.

This allows us to easily derive the desired algorithmic version of
Proposition

9.3
.
Note that using
Lemma

9.5
throughout our arguments in this section would immediately yield a version with a tighter bound on the depth and a slightly worse runtime.

Lemma 9.18
.

Let
q
be an integer with
q\geq 4
and let
(G,\Omega)
be a society.
There exists a crooked transaction of order
q
in
(G,\Omega)
or
(G,\Omega)
has a cylindrical rendition of depth at most
12q+4
.

In particular, there exists an algorithm that returns either the crooked transaction or the cylindrical rendition in time
\mathbf{O}(q|E(G)||V(G)|^{2})
.

Proof.

As noted in
Definition

9.11
, the society
(G,\Omega)
itself trivially describes a
(q+3,0)
-constriction.
This allows us to apply
Corollary

9.13
.
If this yields a cylindrical rendition, we are done.

Otherwise, we find a crooked transaction of order
q
in the frontier of a
(q+3,\ell)
-constriction of
(G,\Omega)
for some non-negative integer
\ell
.
If
\ell=0
, we are done and otherwise
Corollary

9.17
yields the desired crooked transaction of order
q
in
(G,\Omega)
.
∎

10
Orthogonalising crooked transactions

As demonstrated in the last few sections, at the core of many of our proofs is the routing of large linkages through cycles, other linkages, and structures that more or less look like a grid.
This section is devoted to doing this in a more abstracted way than we previously had to.
In particular, we want to find a large orthogonal crooked transaction in societies with a cylindrical society and a large nest and often we also want to manipulate radial linkages associated with these objects.
Note that for these purposes
Proposition

9.3
alone is not a strong enough statement, since it does not respect existing nests and renditions.

The results of this section are the technical underpinnings of the more complex structures and proofs we will present in the remaining sections.
The running times of the implicit algorithms we present here are central to the total running time of our algorithm for finding both the global decomposition and the witnesses for the local structure theorem.
This is a consequence of the fact that the lemmata and algorithms we present here will be used in the context of several iterative processes found in later arguments.

10.1
Orthogonalising radial linkages

The first type of object we will orthogonalise are radial linkages, which builds the basis for our later efforts in this section.
To illustrate why this is of interesting to us, we first present a useful intermediate lemma.
At several points towards establishing the Local Structure Theorem we will have to connect linkages that have been extracted from deeper inside the structure to their respective radial linkages while making sure that enough of this radial linkage still reaches all the way “down” to the vortices.
The following lemma is a consequence of
Menger’s Theorem
and fits exactly this purpose.

Lemma 10.1
.

Let
s,r,k,\ell
be positive integers with
s\geq r+3
.
Let
(G,\Omega)
be a society with a cylindrical rendition
\rho
and a nest
\mathcal{C}=\{C_{1},\ldots,C_{s}\}
around the vortex
c_{0}
.
Moreover, let
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{R}
each be radial linkages of order
r
in
(G,\Omega)
such that both are orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}
and let
I=[\ell,k]\subseteq[2,s]
be an interval with
|I|=r+2
.

Then there exists a radial linkage
\mathcal{P}
of order
r
in
(G,\Omega)
such that

i)

\mathcal{P}
is orthogonal to
\{C_{i}~\!\colon\!~i\in[s]\setminus I\}
with endpoints on
C_{1}
,

ii)

H_{\ell}\cap\bigcup\mathcal{P}
is a subgraph of
H_{\ell}\cap\mathcal{L}
, where
H_{\ell}
is the inner graph of
C_{\ell}
in
\rho
.
In particular, the endpoints of
\mathcal{P}
on
V(C_{1})
coincide with the endpoints of
\mathcal{L}
on
V(C_{1})
, and

iii)

H_{k}\cap\bigcup\mathcal{P}
is a subgraph of
H_{k}\cap\mathcal{R}
, where
H_{k}
is the outer graph of
C_{k}
in
\rho
.
In particular, the endpoints of
\mathcal{P}
on
V(\Omega)
coincide with the endpoints of
\mathcal{R}
on
V(\Omega)
.

Moreover, there exists an algorithm that finds
\mathcal{P}
in time
\mathbf{O}(r|E(G)|)
.

Proof.

Let
(G^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime})
be the
C_{k}
-society in
\rho
and let
X
be the set of all vertices
x
such that
x
is the first vertex in
V(G^{\prime})\cap\pi(N(\rho))
encountered when traversing along some
R\in\mathcal{R}
starting in
V(\Omega)
and let
\rho^{\prime}
be the restriction of
\rho
to
(G^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime})
.
Notice that
X
is a subset of the vertices of the outer graph of
C_{k-1}
.

Moreover, let
G^{\prime\prime}
be the outer graph of
C_{\ell}
in
\rho^{\prime}
.
Finally, let
Y
be the set of all vertices
y
such that
y
is the last vertex of
V(G^{\prime\prime})\cap\pi(N(\rho))
encountered when traversing along some path
L\in\mathcal{L}
starting from
V(\Omega^{\prime})
.
It follows that
Y
is a subset of the vertices of the inner graph of
C_{\ell+1}
.

Notice that
G^{\prime\prime}
has a vortex-free
\Delta
-decomposition, where
\Delta
is a disk, such that

•

the vertices from
X
are grounded and on a common face,

•

the vertices from
Y
are grounded and on a common face, and

•

the cycles in
\{C_{\ell+1},\ldots,C_{k-1}\}
are grounded and the trace of each
C_{i}
with
i\in[\ell+1,k-1]
separates
X
from
Y
in
\Delta
.

Now let
G^{\prime\prime\prime}\coloneqq G^{\prime}\cap G^{\prime\prime}
.
Then, for each
x\in X
there exists
R\in\mathcal{R}
and a path
R^{\prime}\subseteq R\cap G^{\prime\prime\prime}
such that
R^{\prime}
connects
x
and
V(C_{\ell})
.
Similarly, for every
y\in Y
there exists
L\in\mathcal{L}
and a path
L^{\prime}\subseteq L\cap G^{\prime\prime\prime}
such that
L^{\prime}
connects
y
and
V(C_{k})
.

If there exists an
X
-
Y
-linkage of order
r
in
G^{\prime\prime\prime}
, we may combine this linkage with the
V(\Omega)
-
X
-linkage consisting of subpaths of the paths in
\mathcal{R}
contained in the outer graph of
C_{k}
in
\rho
and the
Y
-
V(C_{1})
-linkage consisting of subpaths of
\mathcal{L}
contained in the inner graph of
C_{\ell}
in
\rho
.
This would then result in a
V(\Omega)
-
Y
-linkage as required.
Moreover, recall that via an algorithmic version of
Proposition

2.1
we are able to find such a linkage, if it exists, in time
\mathbf{O}(r|E(G)|)
.

So we may suppose, towards a contradiction, that there does not exist an
X
-
Y
-linkage of order
r
in
G^{\prime\prime}
.
In this case, by
proposition

2.1
, there must exist a set
S\subseteq V(G^{\prime})
of size at most
r-1
such that
G^{\prime\prime}-S
does not contain an
X
-
Y
-path.
However, there must exist
L\in\mathcal{L}
,
R\in\mathcal{R}
, and
i\in[\ell+1,k-1]
such that
H\coloneqq G^{\prime\prime}\cap(L\cup R\cup C_{i})
does not contain a vertex from
S
.
Since both
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{R}
are orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}
it follows that
H
is connected.
Since
V(H)\cap X\neq\emptyset
and
V(H)\cap Y\neq\emptyset
this is a contradiction to the choice of the set
S
.
∎

We can now move on to properly orthogonalising a radial linkage.
Before proving this lemma, we first define a property which we wish to preserve whenever we modify a radial linkage such that it becomes orthogonal to a nest.

Let
(G,\Omega)
be a society, let
\rho
be a cylindrical rendition of
(G,\Omega)
in a disk
\Delta
, and let
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{Q}
be two linkages of the same order.
If for each path
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
there exists a path
P\in\mathcal{P}
with the same endpoints as
Q
, we say that
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{Q}
are
end-identical
.

Lemma 10.2
.

Let
s,r
with
r\leq s
.
Let
(G,\Omega)
be a society with a cylindrical rendition
\rho
around a vortex
c_{0}
with a cozy nest
\mathcal{C}
of order
s
and a radial linkage
\mathcal{R}
of order
r
for
\mathcal{C}
.

Then there exists a radial linkage
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
of order
r
for
\mathcal{C}
that is orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}
and end-identical to
\mathcal{R}
.

Moreover, there exists an algorithm running in
\mathbf{O}(r|E(G)|)
-time that finds
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Note that since
\mathcal{R}
is a radial linkage, there are no non-trivial
C_{1}
-paths in any of the paths in
\mathcal{R}
, as the only vertices used in
\mathcal{R}
that are found in
V(C_{1})
are the endpoints of the paths that do not lie in
V(\Omega)
.
Let the endpoints of the paths in
\mathcal{R}
that lie in
V(\Omega)
be
a_{1},\ldots,a_{r}
found in the given order in
\Omega
, such that
R_{i}
has the endpoint
a_{i}
for all
i\in[r]
.
In the context of this proof, we will always traverse along the paths of our radial linkages starting from their unique endpoint on
V(\Omega)
.

We now inductively construct radial linkages
\mathcal{R}_{s},\dots,\mathcal{R}_{1}
such that for each
i\in[s]
, the radial linkage
\mathcal{R}_{i}
is end-identical to
\mathcal{R}
and orthogonal to the cycles
\{C_{i},\dots,C_{s}\}
.

Let us begin with the construction of
\mathcal{R}_{s}
.
For each
i\in[r]
let
x_{s}^{i}
be the first vertex of
R_{i}\in\mathcal{R}
on
C_{s}
and let
y_{s}^{i}
be its last.
We claim that there exists a subpath
P_{s}^{i}
of
C_{s}
with endpoints
x_{s}^{i}
and
y_{s}^{i}
such that
P_{s}^{i}
is disjoint from all paths
R\in\mathcal{R}\setminus\{R_{i}\}
.

To see this, first let
J_{s}
be the trace of
C_{s}
and
T_{i}
be the trace of the
x_{s}^{i}
-
y_{s}^{i}
-subpath of
R_{i}
in
\rho
.
Since
\mathcal{C}
is cozy and
R_{i}
has exactly one vertex on
C_{1}
, the trace
T_{i}
must be fully contained within the annulus
A_{s}
whose boundary is
J_{s}
and the trace of
C_{1}
.
Moreover, if
\Delta_{s}^{i}
is the
c_{0}
-disk of
J_{s}\cup T_{i}
, then, as
\mathcal{C}
is cozy, every vertex or edge of any path
R\in\mathcal{R}\setminus\{R_{i}\}
that is drawn in
A_{s}
must be drawn in
\Delta_{s}^{i}\setminus T_{i}
.
Suppose this were not the case, then we note that since
\mathcal{C}
is cozy,
A_{s}-\Delta_{s}^{i}
defines a disk whose closure
D_{s}^{i}
is bounded by
T_{i}
and a segment of
J_{s}
.
Should some path
R
from
\mathcal{R}\setminus\{R_{i}\}
reach
D_{s}^{i}
this immediately implies that
\mathcal{C}
is not cozy, since
R
must also reach
C_{1}
and thus, there exists a
C_{s}
-path in the outer graph of
C_{s}
in
R
.

Now observe that there exists a segment
S_{s}^{i}
of
J_{s}
with endpoints
\pi^{-1}(x_{s}^{i})
and
\pi^{-1}(y_{s}^{i})
which is disjoint from
\Delta_{s}^{i}\setminus T_{i}
and thus lies in the boundary of
D_{s}^{i}
.
The subpath of
C_{s}
whose trace is
S_{s}^{i}
is exactly the path
P_{s}^{i}
we desired.
Let us now define
\mathcal{R}_{s}
to be the linkage obtained from
\mathcal{R}
by replacing, for each
i\in[r]
, the
x_{s}^{i}
-
y_{s}^{i}
-subpath of
R_{i}
with the path
P_{s}^{i}
.
Then
\mathcal{R}_{s}
is a radial linkage of order
r
for
\mathcal{C}
which is end-identical to
\mathcal{R}
and the intersection of any path in
\mathcal{R}_{s}
with
C_{s}
is a single, possibly trivial, path.
Hence,
\mathcal{R}_{s}
is orthogonal to
\{C_{s}\}
as required.

Now assume that for some
j\in[3,s]
we have already constructed the linkage
\mathcal{R}_{j}=\{R_{1}^{j},\dots,R_{r}^{j}\}
such that
R_{i}^{j}
has the same endpoints as
R_{i}
for each
i\in[r]
.
Let
J_{j-1}
be the trace of
C_{j-1}
and
A_{j-1}
be the annulus whose boundary is
J_{j-1}
and the trace of
C_{1}
.
Moreover, for each
i\in[r]
let
x_{j-1}^{i}
be the first vertex of
R_{i}^{j}
on
C_{j-1}
,
y_{j-1}^{i}
be its last, and let
T_{i}
be the trace of the
x_{j-1}^{i}
-
y_{j-1}^{i}
-subpath of
R_{i}^{j}
.
It follows from the assumption that
\mathcal{R}_{j}
is orthogonal to
\{C_{j},\dots,C_{s}\}
and
\mathcal{C}
is cozy, that
T_{i}
is contained within
A_{j-1}
.
Indeed, the same arguments as before now show that for each
i\in[r]
there exists a
x_{j-1}^{i}
-
y_{j-1}^{i}
-subpath
P_{j-1}^{i}
of
C_{j-1}
which is disjoint from the paths in
\mathcal{R}_{j}\setminus\{R_{i}^{j}\}
.
Hence, we may define
R_{i}^{j-1}
to be the path obtained from
R_{i}^{j}
by replacing
x_{j-1}^{i}R_{i}^{j}y_{j-1}^{i}
with
P_{j-1}^{i}
for each
i\in[r]
and then set
\mathcal{R}_{j-1}\coloneqq\{R_{j-1}^{i}~\!\colon\!~i\in[r]\}
.

The linkage
\mathcal{R}_{1}
we ultimately arrive at is now the orthogonal, radial linkage for
\mathcal{C}
we searched for and by construction we observe that
\mathcal{R}_{1}
is end-identical to
\mathcal{R}
.
This completes the proof.
∎

10.2
Identifying a crooked witness

In our efforts to find an orthogonal crooked transaction we will of course want to use
Menger’s theorem
.
Using this liberally is however extremely dangerous, as Menger’s theorem can completely alter the character of the transaction we will find.
For example, if we simply took the two end segments of a crooked transaction that we want to orthogonalise and ask
Menger’s theorem
to find us a large transaction between these end segments, the result may be a planar transaction.

Thus, we will now invest quite some effort into finding and preserving witnesses for the fact that the transaction we initially started with was crooked.
For now, we introduce two types of witnesses that certify that we are dealing with a crooked transaction, based on overpasses and doublecrosses introduced in
Section

9.2
.

Definition 10.3

(Crooked witnesses)
.

Let
s,p,j
be positive integers with
2\leq p
and
j\leq s
.
Let
(G,\Omega)
be a society with a cylindrical rendition
\rho
around a vortex
c_{0}
in the disk
\Delta
with a cozy nest
\mathcal{C}=\{C_{1},\ldots,C_{s}\}
of order
s
.
Further, let
\mathcal{P}
be an exposed transaction of order
p
in
(G,\Omega)
and let
(G_{j},\Omega_{j})
be the
C_{j}
-society in
\rho
.

Let
X,Y
be the two end segments of
\mathcal{P}
in
\Omega
and let
X^{\prime}
, respectively
Y^{\prime}
, be the set of vertices in
V(C_{j})\cap\pi(N(\rho))
that appear first when traversing the paths in
\mathcal{P}
from
X
to
Y
, respectively
Y
to
X
.
Note that
X^{\prime}
preserves the order of the paths induced by
X
and the same is true for
Y^{\prime}
with respect to
Y
.
Furthermore, we have
X^{\prime}\cup Y^{\prime}\subseteq V(\Omega_{j})
.
Let
I
be the minimal segment of
\Omega_{j}
that contains
X^{\prime}
and is disjoint from
Y^{\prime}

and let
J
be the minimal segment of
\Omega_{j}
that contains
Y^{\prime}
and is disjoint from
X^{\prime}
.
Further, we let
A,B
be the two distinct, disjoint segments of
\Omega_{j}
that have one endpoint in
X^{\prime}
and the other in
Y^{\prime}
and are otherwise disjoint from
X^{\prime}\cup Y^{\prime}
.

If
R
is an
A
-
B
-path in
G_{j}-V(\bigcup\mathcal{P})
, we call
\{R\}
a
snitch (for
\mathcal{P}
on
C_{j}
)
.
Two
A
-paths
P,P^{\prime}
and two
B
-paths
Q,Q^{\prime}
in
G_{j}-V(\bigcup\mathcal{P})
, such that the pair
P,P^{\prime}
form a cross in
(G_{j},\Omega_{j})
, the pair
Q,Q^{\prime}
form a cross in
(G_{j},\Omega_{j})
, and
P,P^{\prime},Q,Q^{\prime}
are pairwise disjoint, cause us to call
\{P,P^{\prime},Q,Q^{\prime}\}
a
doublecross (for
\mathcal{P}
on
C_{j}
)
(recall
figure

12
for an illustration).
We extend both of these notions in the natural way to snitches and doublecrosses for
\mathcal{P}
on
(G,\Omega)
which have all of their endpoints in
V(\Omega)
.
Whenever there exists a set of paths
\mathcal{W}
that constitute a snitch or a doublecross for
\mathcal{P}
on
C_{j}
(or
\Omega
), we say that there exists a
crooked witness (for
\mathcal{P}
on
C_{j}
(or
(G,\Omega)
))
.

Given a crooked transaction of modest size, it is very easy to find a transaction with a crooked witness in
(G,\Omega)
.

Lemma 10.4
.

Let
q
be a positive integer, let
(G,\Omega)
be a society, and let
\mathcal{Q}
be a crooked transaction of order
5q-4
.

Then
\mathcal{Q}
contains a transaction
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
of order
q
and a crooked witness for
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
in
(G,\Omega)
, which can be found in
\mathbf{O}(q|E(G)|)
-time.

Proof.

Note that
5q-4=4((q+1)-2)+q
.
Thus
Lemma

9.4
either yields a leap of order
q+1
from
\mathcal{Q}
or a doublecross of order
q+4
.
Both options immediately yield the desired transaction
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
of order
q
together with a crooked witness.
∎

We will however instead require a crooked witness deep inside the nest, close to the vortex of the cylindrical rendition.
It is hard to give an intuitive justification for the effort we go through for this purpose at this point.
But such a witness is the key to our efforts of orthogonalising crooked transactions to (most of) a given nest.

Lemma 10.5
.

Let
s,p
be positive integers with
2\leq s\leq p
.
Let
(G,\Omega)
be a society with a cylindrical rendition
\rho=(\Gamma,\mathcal{D})
around a vortex
c_{0}
in the disk
\Delta
with a cozy nest
\mathcal{C}=\{C_{1},\ldots,C_{s}\}
of order
s
.
Further, let
\mathcal{P}
be a crooked transaction of order
8s-14+3p
in
(G,\Omega)
.

Then there exists an exposed transaction
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
of order
p
that has a crooked witness on
C_{2}
.

Furthermore, said transaction and its crooked witness can be found in time
\mathbf{O}(p|E(G)|)
.

Proof.

Let
(G^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime})
be the
C_{2}
-society in
\rho
and let
G_{2}
be the outer graph of
C_{2}
in
\rho
.
Let
X,Y
be the two end segments of
\mathcal{P}
in
\Omega
, with
X^{\prime}
, respectively
Y^{\prime}
, being the set of vertices in
V(C_{2})
that appear first when traversing the paths in
\mathcal{P}
from
X
to
Y
, respectively
Y
to
X
.
As noted in
Definition

10.3
, both
X^{\prime}
and
Y^{\prime}
preserve the order of the paths induced by
X
and respectively
Y
due to
\rho
being a cylindrical rendition.
We let
I
be the minimal segment of
\Omega^{\prime}
that contains
X^{\prime}
and is disjoint from
Y^{\prime}

and let
J
be the minimal segment of
\Omega^{\prime}
that contains
Y^{\prime}
and is disjoint from
X^{\prime}
.
Let
u
be one of the two endpoints of
I
and let
P\in\mathcal{P}
be the path with
u\in V(P)
.

We start with an observation on subpaths of
P
that immediately help us.
Let
A,B
be the two distinct, disjoint segments of
\Omega^{\prime}
that have one endpoint in
I
and the other in
J
and are otherwise disjoint from
I\cup J
, such that
u\in A
.

Claim 10.5.1
.

If
P
contains one of the following types of paths, then we can find a snitch for a subtransaction of
\mathcal{P}
of order
p
:

•

an
A
-
B
-path within
G^{\prime}
that is disjoint from
I\cup J
,

•

a
V(\Omega^{\prime})
-path within
G^{\prime}
whose endpoints define two segments of
\Omega^{\prime}
such that one of them contains all of
I
, respectively all of
J
, and the other segment contains at least
p
vertices of
Y
, respectively
X
, other than the endpoints of
P
, or

•

an
I
-
J
-path within
G^{\prime}
such that for a segment
S
of
\Omega^{\prime}
that has the same endpoints as this path, we have
||X^{\prime}\cap S|-|Y^{\prime}\cap S||\geq p+1
.

Proof of
Claim

10.5.1
:

The fact that any of these paths yields a snitch is immediate from their specifications.

\blacksquare

Next, the following behaviour of
P
with respect to vertices in
(I\cup J)\cap(X^{\prime}\cup Y^{\prime})
is fairly basic but central to our later arguments.
For any
Q\in\mathcal{P}
, let
I_{Q}
be a smallest segment of
\Omega^{\prime}
that minimises the intersection of
I_{Q}
and
B
, contains
A
, and contains all vertices in
V(Q)\cap(I\cup J)
.

Claim 10.5.2
.

For any path
Q\in\mathcal{P}
with
X^{\prime}\cup Y^{\prime}\not\subseteq I_{Q}
, any
V(\Omega^{\prime})
-path
Q^{\prime}
in
Q
whose endpoints separate two vertices in
X^{\prime}\cup Y^{\prime}
from each other in
\Omega^{\prime}
is found in
G^{\prime}
.

Proof of
Claim

10.5.2
:

Let
Q^{\prime}
be such a path and suppose that
Q^{\prime}
is not found in
G^{\prime}
, which implies that it is instead found in
G_{2}
.
We let
a\in X^{\prime}\cup Y^{\prime}
be the vertex within
I_{Q}
that is separated from a vertex
b\in X^{\prime}\cup Y^{\prime}
within
\Omega^{\prime}
by removing the endpoints of
Q^{\prime}
.
Furthermore, we can choose
b
to be outside
I_{Q}
, since
X^{\prime}\cup Y^{\prime}\not\subseteq I_{Q}
.
For each vertex in
X^{\prime}\cup Y^{\prime}
there exists a unique path in
G_{2}\cap\bigcup\mathcal{P}
connecting it to
V(\Omega)
, since the vertices in
X^{\prime}\cup Y^{\prime}
are chosen such that they are the first vertices encountered on a path from
\mathcal{P}
when traversing it from either end.
We note that
Q^{\prime}
must be internally disjoint from all such paths, since it is disjoint from all paths in
\mathcal{P}\setminus\{Q\}
and has both endpoints in
V(\Omega^{\prime})
.
However, since the endpoints of
Q^{\prime}
separate
a
and
b
within
\Omega^{\prime}
and
G_{2}
is grounded in
\rho
, the path
Q^{\prime}
must intersect either the path linking
a
to
V(\Omega)
, or the path linking
b
to
V(\Omega)
, a contradiction.

\blacksquare

Now let us consider the situation in which all interactions between
I\cup J
and
P
occur fairly close to
A
.
We claim that in this situation we can either find a snitch or set ourselves up for finding a doublecross later.
Let us put the second option in more concrete terms and give a few more definitions before we state our claim.

Let
\mathcal{L}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
be a transaction of order
q
and let
R,R^{\prime}
be a cross in
(G^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime})
.
We say that
\mathcal{L}
and
R,R^{\prime}
are a
doublecross-setup of order
q
if the two endpoints of
R,R^{\prime}
are all found in one of the two segments of
\Omega^{\prime}
that result from the deletion of the two minimal segments of
\Omega^{\prime}
that contain all the vertices in
X^{\prime}\cap V(\bigcup\mathcal{L})
, respectively
Y^{\prime}\cap V(\bigcup\mathcal{L})
, and none of the vertices in
Y^{\prime}
, respectively
X^{\prime}
.

Our main focus for the remainder of the proof will be on subpaths of
P
starting in
u
and moving up until they have reached a certain distance in
I\cup J
towards
B
.
We start with a simple fact – which derives from
\mathcal{P}
being crooked – that will prove to be valuable later on.

Since
\mathcal{P}
is crooked, if we let
u^{\prime}\in Y^{\prime}
be the vertex of
V(P)
found in
J
, we know that the segment of
\Omega^{\prime}
that has the endpoints
u,u^{\prime}
and is disjoint from
I\setminus\{u\}
must contain at least one vertex in
Y^{\prime}
other than
u^{\prime}
.
Let
u^{\prime\prime}\in V(uPu^{\prime})\cap(I\cup J)
, then we define
G_{u^{\prime\prime}}
to be the union of
V(\Omega^{\prime})
and
uPu^{\prime\prime}\cap G^{\prime}
.

Claim 10.5.3
.

Let
q
be an integer with
q\leq 2s-3
.
If
|I_{P}\cap(X^{\prime}\cup Y^{\prime})|\leq q
, we can either find an snitch for a subtransaction of
\mathcal{P}
of order
p
, or we can find a doublecross-setup of order at least
(8s-14+3p)-(2q+p-1)
in time
\mathbf{O}(p|E(G)|)
.

Proof of
Claim

10.5.3
:

We note that we have
|I_{P}\cap(X^{\prime}\cup Y^{\prime})|\geq 3
, since
u
and
u^{\prime}
must be contained in
I_{P}
and furthermore, as just noted prior to this claim, there exists a third vertex
v^{\prime}\in Y^{\prime}\cap I_{P}
found in the segment of
\Omega^{\prime}
that contains
A
,
u
, and
u^{\prime}
.
We let
Q
be the path that contains
v^{\prime}
and let
v\in X^{\prime}
be the other vertex of
Q
found in
X^{\prime}\cup Y^{\prime}
.

Suppose that
(G_{u^{\prime}},\Omega^{\prime})
contains a cross consisting of the paths
R,R^{\prime}
, which we can easily check in linear time.
Then the segment of
\Omega^{\prime}
that contains
A
and all endpoints of
R,R^{\prime}
is a subset of
I_{P}
.
Thus, by removing all paths in
\mathcal{P}
that contain a vertex of
I_{P}\cap(X^{\prime}\cup Y^{\prime})
, we can find a transaction of order at least
(8s-14+3p)-(q-1)
that together with
R,R^{\prime}
forms a doublecross-setup of order
(8s-14+3p)-(q-1)
.

Thus we may instead suppose that
(G_{u^{\prime}},\Omega^{\prime})
in fact does not contain a cross.
In particular, this implies that we may imagine
P
as being drawn in a planar fashion within
\Gamma
, such that
P
forms a curve
T
that does not intersect itself, has its endpoints on the boundary of
\Delta
, and is otherwise disjoint from the boundary of
\Delta
.

Consider the closures of the two disks within
\Delta-T
.
Then exactly one of the two disks contains the drawing of
v^{\prime}
.
Let
\Delta_{v^{\prime}}
be the disk containing
v^{\prime}
and let
\Delta_{v}
be the other disk.
Other than the vertices in
V(P)
, each vertex of
G
is drawn on exactly one of these two disks, since
\Gamma
cannot draw vertices outside of
P
onto the points representing the edges in
E(P)
.

Claim

10.5.2
now implies that
\Delta_{v^{\prime}}
does not contain any of the vertices of
X^{\prime}\setminus\{u\}
, which means that
v
is found in
\Delta_{v}
.
(See
Figure

42
for an illustration of this configuration.)

P
\Delta_{v}
\Delta_{v^{\prime}}
A
B
v
v^{\prime}
I
J
u
u^{\prime}

Figure 42
:
An illustration of the situation we arrive in during the proof of
Claim

10.5.3
. The faint yellow disk corresponds to
\Delta_{v}
, while the disk in the middle described by the dotted purple and thicker blue lines is the
C_{2}
-society. The dashed line in black indicates a line that will be followed onto
\Omega
within the context of
Claim

10.5.3
. From the perspective of
Claim

10.5.4
the reader can replace
u^{\prime}
with
u^{\prime\prime}
with the dashed line in black indicating the line that
Q
must pass through.

As a consequence of this, the drawing of some
V(\Omega^{\prime})
-path within
vQv^{\prime}
in
\Gamma
must cross
T
.
Note that
P
contains two disjoint paths connecting both
u
and
u^{\prime}
to
V(\Omega)
within
G_{2}
.
These two paths separate
v
and
v^{\prime}
in
G_{2}
, since
P
and
Q
are disjoint.
In particular,
u
and
u^{\prime}
separate
v
and
v^{\prime}
within
\Omega^{\prime}
thanks to our choice of
v^{\prime}
and the fact that
u
is an endpoint of
I
.
Thus there exists some pair of paths
P^{\prime}\subseteq uPu^{\prime}
and
Q^{\prime}\subseteq vQv^{\prime}
such that
P^{\prime},Q^{\prime}
form a cross in
(G^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime})
.

Note that
Q^{\prime}
must have at least one endpoint in
I_{P}
.
Thanks to
Claim

10.5.1
, we know that the minimal segment
I^{*}
of
\Omega^{\prime}
that contains
I_{P}
and all endpoints of
Q^{\prime}
either contains at most
2q+p
vertices from
X^{\prime}\cup Y^{\prime}
or
Q^{\prime}
is a snitch for a subtransaction of
\mathcal{P}
of order
p
.
In the second case, we are done.
Otherwise, by removing the paths corresponding to the at most
2q+p
endpoints in
I^{*}
– two of which belong to
P
– we can use
P^{\prime},Q^{\prime}
to form a doublecross-setup of order
(8s-14+3p)-(2q+p-1)
.
This proves our claim and following the arguments of our proof yields a procedure that finds the desired witnesses in time
\mathbf{O}(p|E(G)|)
.

\blacksquare

In general there is no guarantee that we are given a chance to apply
Claim

10.5.3
.
Thus we will have to argue that we can find a snitch or a doublecross-setup if
P
wanders too far away from
A
.

Claim 10.5.4
.

If
|I_{P}\cap(X^{\prime}\cup Y^{\prime})|\geq 2s-3
, we can either find a snitch for a subtransaction of
\mathcal{P}
of order
p
, or we can find a doublecross-setup of order
(8s-14+3p)-(2s-3+p)
.

Proof of
Claim

10.5.4
:

Let
u^{\prime\prime}\in V(P)\cap(I\cup J)
be the vertex minimising the length of
uPu^{\prime\prime}
such that the smallest segment
I_{u^{\prime\prime}}
of
\Omega^{\prime}
that contains all vertices of
uPu^{\prime\prime}
also contains at least
2s-3
vertices from
X^{\prime}\cup Y^{\prime}
.
Note that this may overshoot our goal of
2s-3
vertices by at most
p
vertices according to
Claim

10.5.1
.
If
(G_{u^{\prime\prime}},\Omega^{\prime})
contains a cross, we can therefore find a snitch or a doublecross-setup of the appropriate order using the same methods as in our proof of
Claim

10.5.3
.
Thus we may instead suppose that
(G_{u^{\prime\prime}},\Omega^{\prime})
does not contain a cross.

Let
X^{\prime\prime}=(I_{u^{\prime\prime}}\cap X^{\prime})\setminus\{u\}
and let
Y^{\prime\prime}=I_{u^{\prime\prime}}\cap Y^{\prime}
.
By pigeonhole principle, since
I_{u^{\prime\prime}}
contains at least
2s-3
vertices of
X^{\prime}\cup Y^{\prime}
, one of the sets
X^{\prime\prime}
or
Y^{\prime\prime}
contains at least
s-1
vertices.
W.l.o.g. we let this set be
X^{\prime\prime}
.
Let
w
be the endpoint of
I_{u^{\prime\prime}}
that lies in
I
and note that thus
uIw
contains
X^{\prime\prime}
.
This further implies that
uPw
is a subpath of
P
such that the smallest segment of
\Omega^{\prime}
that contains all vertices of
uPw
also contains
X^{\prime\prime}
.

Similar to the proof of
Claim

10.5.3
, we note that we can assume that
Pw
is drawn by
\Gamma
as a curve
T
in
\Delta
that does not intersect itself and has one end on the boundary of
\Delta
, the other end on the boundary of
c_{0}
and such that
T
is disjoint from the boundary of
\Delta
with the exception of one of its endpoints.
For each
x\in X^{\prime\prime}
let
x^{\prime}
be the vertex of
Y^{\prime}
that is found in the same path of
\mathcal{P}
to which
x
belongs and let
X^{*}
be the collection of these vertices.
Let
x\in X^{\prime\prime}
be chosen arbitrarily, let
Q\in\mathcal{P}
be the path with
x\in V(Q)
, and let
x^{\prime}\in V(Q)\cap X^{*}
.

Thanks to
Claim

10.5.2
and as
x\in X^{\prime\prime}
, for
Q
to reach
x^{\prime}\in J
, the drawing of
Q
in
\Gamma
must either cross
T
within
c_{0}
and thus verify the existence of a cross in
(G^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime})
consisting of two paths
P^{\prime}\subseteq Pu_{i}
and
Q^{\prime}\subseteq Q
, or there exists a
V(\Omega^{\prime})
-path
Q^{\prime\prime}
in
Q
that is found in
G_{2}
such that there exists a segment of
\Omega^{\prime}
with the same endpoints as
Q^{\prime\prime}
that contains
w
and is disjoint from
A
,
B
, and
J
.

For the second option, as
\mathcal{C}
is cozy, we note that this path must intersect some cycle
C\in\mathcal{C}\setminus\{C_{1},C_{2}\}
such that
Q^{\prime\prime}
does not contain any non-trivial
C
paths in the outer graph of
C
in
\rho
.
Thus, there can exist at most
s-2
vertices in
X^{\prime\prime}
for which the second option holds.
Since
|X^{\prime\prime}|>s-2
, there must therefore exist some vertex in
X^{\prime\prime}
for which the first option is true.
Hence, we may assume w.l.o.g. that the cross in
(G^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime})
consisting of paths
P^{\prime}\subseteq Pw
and
Q^{\prime}\subseteq Q
exists.

Via an entirely analogous argument to the one presented in our proof of
Claim

10.5.3
, we can now see that either
Q^{\prime}
is a snitch for some subtransaction of
\mathcal{P}
of order
p
, or there exists a doublecross-setup, with
P^{\prime},Q^{\prime}
being the cross, for a transaction of order at least
8s-14+3p-(2s-3+p)
.
This proves our claim.

\blacksquare

We may therefore restrict ourselves to the case in which we have somehow found a doublecross-setup of order at least
(8s-14+3p)-(4s-7+p)
as a result of applying
Claim

10.5.4
or
Claim

10.5.3
.
Let
P^{\prime},Q^{\prime}
be the cross within this doublecross-setup and let
\mathcal{L}
be the transaction of order
4s-7+2p
involved in it.

We now want to reapply our arguments from
Claim

10.5.4
and
Claim

10.5.3
to
\mathcal{L}
from the perspective of
B
.
For this purpose we use the endpoint
w\in B
of
I
that is distinct from
u
for this purpose instead of
u
.
Note that
w
may be a vertex of a path
R\in\mathcal{P}
with
P^{\prime}\subseteq R
or
Q^{\prime}\subseteq R
and thus in particular, it may be the case that
R\not\in\mathcal{L}
.
However, we require the fact that
R
cannot be a peripheral path of
\mathcal{P}
to be able to apply
Claim

10.5.3
.
So we simply add
R
back into the doublecross-setup if it was previously deleted.

This clearly does not cause a problem if
Claim

10.5.4
or
Claim

10.5.3
return a snitch.
If they instead return a doublecross-setup, then we note that this doublecross-setup has order at least
p=(4s-7+2p)-(4s-7+p)
.
Let
P^{\prime\prime},Q^{\prime\prime}
be the second cross we found in this process.
Note that the endpoints of
P^{\prime\prime},Q^{\prime\prime}
must be found in a segment of
\Omega^{\prime}
that contains
B
and at most
4s-7+p
vertices of
X^{\prime}\cup Y^{\prime}
.
Similarly, the endpoints of
P^{\prime},Q^{\prime}
are found in a segment of
\Omega^{\prime}
that contains
A
and at most
4s-7+p
vertices of
X^{\prime}\cup Y^{\prime}
.
Thus, since
p
is a positive integer and we started with a transaction of order
8s-14+3p
, the second cross
P^{\prime\prime},Q^{\prime\prime}
must be disjoint from
P^{\prime},Q^{\prime}
and we have therefore actually found our desired doublecross.
∎

One can generalise the proof of this lemma in a straightforward way to find crooked witnesses on any given cycle
C_{j}
of the nest, if we start with a crooked transaction of order
8s-(4j+2)+3p
.

10.3
Orthogonalising transactions

We now move on to presenting a surprisingly simple method that allows us to orthogonalise a given transaction to all but the innermost cycle of the nest.
The reason why this does not immediately solve all our problems is that, as mentioned earlier, this process does not preserve the type of a transaction.

Lemma 10.6
.

Let
s,p
be positive integers.
Let
(G,\Omega)
be a society with a cylindrical rendition
\rho
around a vortex
c_{0}
in the disk
\Delta
with a cozy nest
\mathcal{C}=\{C_{1},\ldots,C_{s}\}
of order
s
.
Further, let
\mathcal{P}
be an exposed transaction of order
s(p-1)+1
in
(G,\Omega)
with the end segments
X_{1},X_{2}
.

Then there exists an exposed transaction
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
of order
p
such that

i)

\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
is orthogonal to
\{C_{2},C_{3},\dots,C_{s}\}
,

ii)

\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
connects vertices of
X_{1}\cap V(\mathcal{P})
to vertices of
X_{2}\cap V(\mathcal{P})
, and

iii)

the intersection of
\bigcup\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
with the union of the inner graph of
C_{1}
in
\rho
and
C_{1}
is fully contained in
C_{1}\cup\bigcup\mathcal{P}
.

Moreover, there exists an algorithm that finds
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
in time
\mathbf{O}(p|E(G)|)
.

Proof.

Let
G^{\prime}
be the union of
C_{1}
and the intersection of the inner graph of
C_{1}
in
\rho
with
\bigcup\mathcal{P}
.
We define two radial linkages
\mathcal{L}_{1}
and
\mathcal{L}_{2}
as follows.
For
i\in[2]
and every
P\in\mathcal{P}
, let
P_{i}
be the unique
X_{i}
-
V(C_{1})
-subpath of
P
.
We set
\mathcal{L}_{i}\coloneqq\{P_{i}~\!\colon\!~P\in\mathcal{P}\}
.
Let
X_{i}^{\prime}
be the set of all endpoints of the paths in
\mathcal{L}_{i}
on
C_{1}
.
Notice that
\mathcal{L}_{1}\cup\mathcal{L}_{2}
is also a radial linkage.

Now suppose there exists a linkage
\mathcal{Q}
of order
p
from
X_{1}^{\prime}
to
X_{2}^{\prime}
in
G^{\prime}
.
By using an algorithmic version of
proposition

2.1
for finding
p
paths, such a linkage can be found in time
\mathbf{O}(p|E(G)|)
.
Let
\mathcal{L}
be the collection of all paths from
\mathcal{L}_{1}\cup\mathcal{L}_{2}
that share an endpoint with some path in
\mathcal{Q}
.
We now apply
lemma

10.2
to the nest
\mathcal{C}
and the radial linkage
\mathcal{L}
to obtain a radial linkage
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
such that
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
are end-identical, and
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
is orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}
.
For each path
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
, let
Q^{\prime}
be the union of
Q
and the two paths from
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
that share an endpoint with
Q
.
Finally, let
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
be the collection of all such paths
Q^{\prime}
.
It follows that
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
is an exposed transaction of order
p
in
(G,\Omega)
and
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
is orthogonal to
\{C_{2},C_{3},\dots,C_{s}\}
.
This is the outcome claimed in the assertion and thus, in this case, we are done.

So now we may assume that there does not exist an
X_{1}^{\prime}
-
X_{2}^{\prime}
-linkage of order
p
in
G^{\prime}
.
By
proposition

2.1
there exists an
X_{1}^{\prime}
-
X_{2}^{\prime}
-separation
(A,B)
of order at most
p-1
in
G^{\prime}
.
Let
S\coloneqq A\cap B
.

For every
P\in\mathcal{P}
, let
\widetilde{P}
be the unique
X_{1}^{\prime}
-
X_{2}^{\prime}
-subpath of
P
.
Let
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}
be the collection of all paths
\widetilde{P}
as defined above.

Let
F
be a
V(C_{1})
-path in
G
that sticks out away from
c_{0}
and let
F^{\prime}
be the unique subpath of
C_{1}
such that
F^{\prime}
has the same endpoints as
F
and
F\cup F^{\prime}
is the unique cycle in
C_{1}\cup F
whose trace does not separate
c_{0}
and the boundary of
\Delta
.
We call
F^{\prime}
the
gangplank
of
F
.
Let
\widetilde{P}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}
.
A subpath
F^{\prime}
of
C_{1}
is a
gangplank
of
\widetilde{P}
if there exists a subpath
F
of
\widetilde{P}
such that
F^{\prime}
is the gangplank of
F
.

We observe that
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}
is an
X_{1}^{\prime}
-
X_{2}^{\prime}
-linkage in
G
of order
s(p-1)+1
.
Hence, there must exist a linkage
\widehat{\mathcal{P}}\subseteq\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}
of order
(s-1)(p-1)+1
that avoids the
p-1
vertices in
S
.
Since
\mathcal{P}
is exposed, every path in
\widehat{\mathcal{P}}
must contain an edge which is drawn in the interior of
c_{0}
and thus has at least one gangplank.

Let
S^{\prime}\coloneqq S\cap V(C_{1})
.
Suppose there exists a path
\widehat{P}\in\widehat{\mathcal{P}}
such that all of its gangplanks avoid
S^{\prime}
.
Let
J
be the union of
G^{\prime}\cap\widehat{P}
and all gangplanks of
\widehat{P}
.
Then
J
must be a connected subgraph of
G^{\prime}
that avoids
S
but contains a vertex of
X_{1}^{\prime}
and a vertex of
X_{2}^{\prime}
.
This is a contradiction to the choice of
S
.
Hence, every path
\widehat{P}\in\widehat{\mathcal{P}}
has a gangplank that contains some vertex of
S^{\prime}
.
With
|S^{\prime}|\leq p-1
and
|\widehat{\mathcal{P}}|\geq(s-1)(p-1)+1
there must exist some
u\in S^{\prime}
such that
u
is contained in a gangplank of
s
paths
\widehat{P}_{1},\dots,\widehat{P}_{s}\in\widehat{\mathcal{P}}
.
For each
i\in[s]
, let
F_{i}
be a subpath of
\widehat{P}_{i}
such that
u
belongs to the gangplank
F^{\prime}_{i}
of
F_{i}
.

The trace of
F_{i}\cap F_{i}^{\prime}
bounds a disk
\Delta_{i}\subseteq\Delta
that does not contain
c_{0}
since
F_{i}
sticks out away from
c_{0}
, for each
i\in[s]
.
Moreover, since the paths from
\widehat{\mathcal{P}}
are pairwise vertex-disjoint, so are the paths
F_{1},\ldots,F_{s}
.
It follows that for all distinct
i,j\in[s]
we have either
\Delta_{i}\subsetneq\Delta_{j}
or
\Delta_{j}\subsetneq\Delta_{i}
.
Thus, we may assume that the paths
F_{1},\ldots,F_{s}
are numbered such that
\Delta_{1}\subsetneq\Delta_{2}\subsetneq\dots\subsetneq\Delta_{s}
.

Since
\mathcal{C}
is cozy, there cannot exist an
i\in[s]
and a
j\in[s]
such that
F_{i}
contains a
C_{j}
-path that sticks out away from
c_{0}
and is disjoint from the cycles in
\{C_{j^{\prime}}~\!\colon\!~j^{\prime}\in[j+1,s]\}
.
It follows that for every
i\in[s]
there exists a
j_{i}\in[2,s]
such that
j_{i}
is maximal with the property that
C_{j_{i}}
contains a vertex of
F_{i}
and every non-trivial
V(C_{j_{i}})
-subpath of
F_{i}
sticks out towards
c_{0}
.
Hence, the trace of
C_{j_{i}}
can intersect the disk
\Delta_{i}
only in its boundary.
However, for any
i^{\prime}<i
the disk
\Delta_{i^{\prime}}
cannot contain a point from the boundary of
\Delta_{i}
.
Thus,
i^{\prime}<i
implies
j_{i^{\prime}}<j_{i}
.
Since
s>s-1=|[2,s]|
, this is absurd.
Hence, this case cannot occur and our proof is complete.
∎

The first situation in which we will need the above lemma will require us to additionally protect a crooked witness.
This results in a slightly more technical statement which has a proof that is very close to that of
Lemma

10.6
.
We include
Lemma

10.6
and its proof here because we believe that it – and in particular its proof – may be of independent interest to readers and we will need a further variant of it later on.
Thus we only sketch the proof of the following lemma.

Lemma 10.7
.

Let
s,p
be positive integers with
s\geq 3
.
Let
(G,\Omega)
be a society with a cylindrical rendition
\rho
around a vortex
c_{0}
in the disk
\Delta
with a cozy nest
\mathcal{C}=\{C_{1},\ldots,C_{s}\}
of order
s
.
Further, let
\mathcal{P}
be an exposed transaction of order
(p+7)(s-2)+p
in
(G,\Omega)
with the end segments
X_{1},X_{2}
and let there exist a crooked witness
\mathcal{W}
for
\mathcal{P}
on
C_{2}
.

Then there exists an exposed transaction
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
of order
p
such that

i)

\mathcal{W}
is a crooked witness for
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
on
C_{2}
,

ii)

\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
is orthogonal to
\{C_{3},C_{4},\dots,C_{s}\}
, and

iii)

\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
connects vertices of
X_{1}\cap V(\mathcal{P})
to vertices of
X_{2}\cap V(\mathcal{P})
.

Moreover, there exists an algorithm that finds
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
in time
\mathbf{O}(p|E(G)|)
.

Proof sketch.

Suppose that
\mathcal{P}
has a doublecross
\{Q_{1},Q_{2},Q_{3},Q_{4}\}
, with
Q_{1},Q_{2}
and
Q_{3},Q_{4}
respectively forming crosses in the
C_{2}
-society
(G_{2},\Omega_{2})
.
For each
i\in[4]
, let
x_{i},x_{i}^{\prime}
be the two distinct endpoints of
Q_{i}
.
Otherwise
\mathcal{P}
has a snitch.
In this case, we call the corresponding path
Q_{1}
, and let
x_{1},x_{1}^{\prime}
be defined as above, with
x_{i}:=x_{1}
,
x_{i}^{\prime}:=x_{1}^{\prime}
, and
Q_{i}:=Q_{1}
for all
i\in[2,4]
.

Let
G^{\prime\prime}\coloneqq C_{2}\cup G_{2}
, let
Z\coloneqq\{x_{i},x_{i}^{\prime}~\!\colon\!~i\in[4]\}
, and note that
Z=V(C_{2})\cap V(\bigcup_{i=1}^{4}Q_{i})
.
We let
G^{\prime}
be
G^{\prime\prime}-(V(\bigcup_{i=1}^{4}Q_{i})\setminus Z)
.

We construct two radial linkages
\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2}
orthogonal to
\{C_{3},C_{4},\dots,C_{s}\}
from the paths in
\mathcal{P}
as in the proof of
Lemma

10.6
and let
X_{1}^{\prime},X_{2}^{\prime}
be the endpoints of these linkages in
V(C_{2})
.
Let
\widetilde{P}
be the
X_{1}^{\prime}
-
X_{2}^{\prime}
-path within
P
, for each
P\in\mathcal{P}
, and let
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}
be the collection of these paths.

If there exists a linkage
\mathcal{Q}
of order
p
from
X_{1}^{\prime}
to
X_{2}^{\prime}
in
G^{\prime}
, we proceed exactly as in the proof of
Lemma

10.6
and thus find the desired transaction in time
\mathbf{O}(p|E(G)|)
.
Therefore we need to argue that the initial size of
\mathcal{P}
is sufficient to guarantee the existence of such a linkage
\mathcal{Q}
.

Suppose
\mathcal{Q}
does not exist, let
(A,B)
be an
X_{1}^{\prime}
-
X_{2}^{\prime}
-separator of order at most
p-1
, which must exists according to
Proposition

2.1
, and let
S:=(A\cap B)\cup Z
.
Note that
|Z|\leq 8
.
Thus we have
|S|\leq p+7
, since
(A,B)
is a separator of order at most
p-1
.
Hence there exists a linkage
\widehat{\mathcal{P}}\subseteq\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}
of order at least
(p+7)(s-2)+1
that avoids
S
, as
Z
is disjoint from
\mathcal{P}
to begin with.
Since each of the paths in
\mathcal{P}
is exposed, each path in
\widehat{\mathcal{P}}
has at least one gangplank.
Let
S^{\prime}:=S\cap V(C_{2})
.

We define
gangplanks
for
V(C_{2})
-paths that stick out away from
c_{0}
and paths
\widetilde{P}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}
as in the proof of
Lemma

10.6
.
Once more, we conclude that there does not exist a path
\widehat{P}\in\widehat{\mathcal{P}}
such that all of its gangplanks avoid
S^{\prime}
.
Hence, every path
\widehat{P}\in\widehat{\mathcal{P}}
has a gangplank that contains some vertex of
S^{\prime}
.
As
|S^{\prime}|\leq p+7
and
|\widehat{\mathcal{P}}|\geq(p+7)(s-2)+1
, there exists some
u\in S^{\prime}
that is contained in a gangplank of
s-1
paths
\widehat{P}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{P}_{s-1}\in\widehat{\mathcal{P}}
.
From here we can then reach a contradiction in just the same fashion as we did in the proof of
Lemma

10.6
.
∎

Note that similar to
Lemma

10.5
, this lemma could be generalised to allow for us to find a crooked witness on
C_{j}
assuming we start with a transaction of order
(p+7)(s-j)+p
.

10.4
Orthogonalising a crooked transaction

We are now almost ready to prove the main theorem of this section.
Roughly described, it will find a crooked transaction in a society with a cylindrical rendition and a nest which is orthogonal to all but a constant number of cycles in the nest.
Thus it is somewhat of an analogue to
Lemma

9.18
, although our theorem will also preserve the existing cylindrical rendition – and thus the nest – to a large degree.

Kawarabayashi et al. also need such a theorem and we have in fact already cited it earlier.
The full version of
Proposition

3.6
(see Lemma 4.5 in
[
KTW21
]
) takes the same role as the theorem we are about to prove.
However, it causes the loss of a number of cycles from the nest that is
linear
in the size of the crooked transaction we want.
As long as the size of the crooked transaction we require is a fixed value, this would be fine for our approach (see
Lemma

3.5
).
However, the crooked transactions we want have a size which itself depends on the
K_{t}
-minor we exclude and thus using their lemma would cause the loss of large numbers of cycles each time we apply it.
Using the lemma from
[
KTW21
]
would therefore ultimately cause our functions to be exponential.

In reality we will need to generalise the main theorem of this section quite a bit more for our applications (see the start of
Section

11
).
We choose to split this presentation because the proof of
Theorem

11.5
adds another considerable layer of technical complexity comparable to the already very technical nature of the proof of
Theorem

10.11
.
By first proving the result in a slightly simpler setting, we hope to aid the reader in understanding our arguments later on in the proof of
Theorem

11.5
.

Let us now present a last technical prerequisite for our proof, before we get into the meat of the argument.
The following very technical lemma concerns the drawing of paths in cylindrical renditions with cozy nests.
It captures the intuition that in a cylindrical rendition with a cozy nest, we cannot find two paths that are nested and sit between two cycles of the nest (see
figure

43
for an illustration).
This is not entirely true, but does hold in most situations.

C_{1}
C_{2}
\Omega
S
Q^{\prime}
R^{\prime}

Figure 43
:
Two paths
R^{\prime}
and
Q^{\prime}
(in
yellow
and
green
respectively) interacting with a nest
\mathcal{C}=\{C_{1},C_{2}\}
in a way that constitutes a violation of
\mathcal{C}
being cozy.
To see this, notice how
Q^{\prime}
cannot leave the segment
S
(depicted in
orange
) of
C_{2}
without producing a
C_{2}
-path that sticks our (towards
\Omega
).
This intuition is captured in
lemma

10.8
.

Lemma 10.8
.

Let
p
be an integer with
p\geq 2
.
Let
(G,\Omega)
be a society with a cylindrical rendition
\rho
around a vortex
c_{0}
with a cozy nest
\mathcal{C}=\{C_{1},C_{2}\}
and let
\mathcal{P}
be an exposed transaction of order
p
in
(G,\Omega)
with the distinct end segments
X,Y
.
Further, let
X^{\prime}
be the set of vertices in
V(\bigcup\mathcal{P})\cap V(C_{2})
that are seen first when traversing the paths in
\mathcal{P}
from
X
to
Y
and let
Y^{\prime}
be defined analogously for traversals from
Y
to
X
.
Let
C_{X}\subseteq C_{2}
and
C_{Y}\subseteq C_{2}
be the two minimal, disjoint paths in
C_{2}
such that
Z\subseteq V(C_{Z})
for both
Z\in\{X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}\}
.

Then for any pair of distinct paths
R,Q\in\mathcal{P}
and any two
C_{2}
-subpaths
R^{\prime}\subseteq R
and
Q^{\prime}\subseteq Q
found in the union of
C_{1}
and the outer graph of
C_{1}
in
\rho
, if both
R^{\prime}
and
Q^{\prime}
each have at least one endpoint outside of
C_{X}\cup C_{Y}
, then the
c_{0}
-disk of
C_{2}\cup R^{\prime}
contains the trace of
Q^{\prime}
in
\rho
and vice versa.

Proof.

Let
R,Q\in\mathcal{P}
be distinct paths and let
R^{\prime}\subseteq R
and
Q^{\prime}\subseteq Q
be two
C_{2}
-subpaths found in the union of
C_{1}
and the outer graph of
C_{1}
in
\rho
such that both
R^{\prime}
and
Q^{\prime}
each have at least one endpoint outside of
C_{X}\cup C_{Y}
.
Let
v\in V(Q^{\prime})\setminus V(C_{X}\cup C_{Y})
be one of these endpoints.

Suppose the
c_{0}
-disk
d
of
C_{2}\cup R^{\prime}
does not contain the trace of
Q^{\prime}
in
\rho
.
Let
C^{\prime}
be the cycle in
C_{2}\cup R^{\prime}
whose
c_{0}
-disk in
\rho
is
d
.
Let
w_{X}\in V(Q)\cap X^{\prime}
and let
w_{Y}\in V(Q)\cap Y^{\prime}
.

Suppose that both
w_{X}
and
w_{Y}
are contained in the unique
C^{\prime}
-path
L
in
C_{2}
of length at least one, which has the property that
C^{\prime}\cup L=C_{2}\cup R^{\prime}
.
As
Q
is exposed, it must contain an edge of
\sigma(c_{0})
.
To reach
c_{0}
from
w_{X}
and
w_{Y}
, the path
Q
must intersect
C^{\prime}
, since
C^{\prime}
separates the internal vertices of
L
from
\sigma(c_{0})
.
Because
Q\neq R
, this means that
Q
must intersect
C^{\prime}-V(R^{\prime})
.
However, this implies the existence of a
C_{2}
-path in
Q
that sticks out away from
c_{0}
, contradicting the coziness of
\mathcal{C}
.

We may therefore suppose that
w_{X}
or
w_{Y}
are not found in
L
.
W.l.o.g. we assume that
w_{Y}
lies outside of
L
.
Since the endpoint
v\in V(Q^{\prime})
is not contained in
V(C_{X}\cup C_{Y})
the path
vQw_{Y}
must somehow reach
C^{\prime}-R^{\prime}
.
This once more implies the existence of a
C_{2}
-path in
Q
that sticks out away from
c_{0}
, contradicting the coziness of
\mathcal{C}
.
Thus the
c_{0}
-disk of
C_{2}\cup R^{\prime}
must contain the trace of
Q^{\prime}
in
\rho
.

The case in which
c_{0}
-disk of
C_{2}\cup Q^{\prime}
does not contain the trace of
R^{\prime}
in
\rho
can then be resolved analogously, completing our proof.
∎

We are now ready for the proof of our main theorem, which we split into three parts.
The first represents the interesting technical core of our argument, involving a quite fascinating discussion of how degenerately the strip of a planar, flat transaction can behave towards a cylindrical rendition if it is not orthogonal to it.
The second part will be the technical version of the statement, we later need in our application.
We mainly state the third part to bring our main theorem into a form resembling
Lemma

9.18
.
The proofs of both the second and third part will be comparatively brief and merciful.

Lemma 10.9
.

Let
p
be integers with
p\geq 4
and let
(G,\Omega)
be a society with a cylindrical rendition
\rho
with a cozy nest
\mathcal{C}=\{C_{1},C_{2}\}
around a vortex
c_{0}
in the disk
\Delta
.
Further, let there exist a planar, flat, isolated, separating transaction
\mathcal{P}
of order
26p+25
with a crooked witness
\mathcal{W}
on
C_{2}
, such that
V(\Omega)
consists of the endpoints of
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{P}
is flat on one side.

Then there exists a crooked transaction
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
of order
p
in
(G,\Omega)
with
\bigcup\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\subseteq\bigcup\mathcal{P}\cup C_{1}\cup C_{2}
.
Moreover, there exists an algorithm that finds
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
in time
\mathbf{O}(ps|E(G)||V(G)|^{2})
.

Proof.

Let
p^{\prime}\coloneqq 26p+25
and let
\mathcal{P}=\{P_{1},\ldots,P_{p^{\prime}}\}
be indexed naturally.
As a consequence of
\mathcal{P}
being flat on one side, we may assume that the labels of the paths in
\mathcal{P}
are chosen such that the following society
(H,\Psi)
has a vortex-free rendition
\rho^{\prime}
into a disk
\Delta^{\prime}
.
Let
I
be the unique segment of
\Omega
that has the same endpoints as
P_{p^{\prime}-1}
and does not contain the endpoints of
P_{p^{\prime}}
.
Further, let
H^{\prime\prime}
be the graph consisting of
\mathcal{P}\setminus\{P_{p^{\prime}}\}
and the vertices in
I
, with
H
being the union of
H^{\prime\prime}
and all
H^{\prime\prime}
-bridges in
G
with at least one attachment in
V(H^{\prime\prime})\setminus V(P_{p^{\prime}-1})
.
As noted in
Definition

9.8
,
H
must be disjoint from both
P_{p^{\prime}}
and the vertices in
V(\Omega)\setminus I
.
The society
(H,\Psi)
that we mentioned above is then defined by choosing
\Psi
such that
V(\Psi)=I\cup(\pi(N(\rho))\cap V(P_{p^{\prime}-1}))
, with the order on
I
