being obtained naturally from
\Omega
and the order of
\pi(N(\rho))\cap V(P_{p^{\prime}-1})
being derived from a traversal of
P_{p^{\prime}-1}
.

For each
i\in[p^{\prime}]
let
x_{i},y_{i}
be the two distinct endpoints of
P_{i}
, such that the two end segments
X,Y
of
\mathcal{P}
respectively contain all
x_{i}
s and all
y_{i}
s for the paths in
\mathcal{P}
.
Let
x_{i}^{\prime}
be the first vertex in
V(C_{2})\cap V(P_{i})
that is seen when traversing
P_{i}
starting from
x_{i}
, with
y_{i}^{\prime}
being defined analogously for a traversal starting from
y_{i}
.
Further, let
P_{i}^{1}
be the maximal subpath of
P_{i}\cap C_{2}
containing
x_{i}^{\prime}
and let
P_{i}^{2}
be the maximal subpath of
P_{i}\cap C_{2}
containing
y_{i}^{\prime}
.

\cdots
C_{N}
C_{S}
C_{W}
C_{E}
P_{p^{\prime}}
P_{1}

:
The transaction
\mathcal{P}
(the

. Here we depict a rendition
\psi
of
(G,\Omega)
such that the restriction of

C_{N}
and

of

are depicted in

and the subpaths

of

are depicted in
purple
.
The drawing indicates a possibility for the forced behaviour of
C_{W}
and
C_{E}
due to the presence of the crooked witness
\mathcal{W}
.

We divide up most of
C_{2}
into four sections as follows (see
figure

44
for an illustration):

•

Let
C_{N}
be the
P_{1}^{1}
-
P_{p^{\prime}}^{1}
-path in
C_{2}
with
P_{j}^{1}\subseteq C_{N}
for all
j\in[2,p^{\prime}-1]
,

•

let
C_{E}
be the
P_{p^{\prime}}^{1}
-

-path in
C_{2}
with
V(P_{j}^{k})\cap V(C_{E})=\emptyset
for all
j\in[p^{\prime}-1]
and
k\in[2]
,

•

let
C_{S}
be the
P_{p^{\prime}}^{2}
-
P_{1}^{2}
-path in
C_{2}
with
P_{j}^{2}\subseteq C_{S}
for all
j\in[2,p^{\prime}-1]
, and

•

let
C_{W}
be the
P_{1}^{2}
-
P_{1}^{1}
-path in
C_{2}
with
V(P_{j}^{k})\cap V(C_{W})=\emptyset
for all
j\in[2,p^{\prime}]
and
k\in[2]
.

Note that by construction
H
contains the two endpoints of
C_{W}
but not those of
C_{E}
.
The names of these pieces of
C_{2}
are chosen to represent cardinal directions to help orient the reader (see also
figure

44
).
We remark that if
C_{2}
was orthogonal to
\mathcal{P}
, neither
C_{E}
nor
C_{W}
could intersect any paths in
\mathcal{P}\setminus\{P_{1},P_{p^{\prime}}\}
.
Furthermore, the endpoints of the paths in
\mathcal{W}
must be found on
C_{E}
and
C_{W}
.
In light of these remarks, we claim the following.
Recall that
p^{\prime}=26p+25
.

Claim 10.9.1
.

At least one of the paths
C_{E}
and
C_{W}
intersects all paths in
\{P_{1},\ldots,P_{13p+12}\}
or
\{P_{13p+13},\ldots,P_{p^{\prime}-1}\}
.

Proof of
Claim

10.9.1
:

Depending on whether
\mathcal{W}
is a snitch or a doublecross, we distinguish two cases.
We start in the case in which
\mathcal{W}
is a snitch and thus
\mathcal{W}=\{P\}
for some
C_{2}
-path
P
found in the inner graph of
C_{2}
in
\rho
.
Recall that
P
is disjoint from all paths in
\mathcal{P}
and this also tells us that
P
has one endpoint in
V(C_{W})
and the other in
V(C_{E})
.
Thus if
P\not\subseteq H
, then it must be disjoint from
H
, as
(H,\Psi)
has a vortex-free rendition
\rho^{\prime}
in a disk
\Delta^{\prime}
.
In particular, this means that
C_{W}
must intersect all paths in
\mathcal{P}\setminus\{P_{p^{\prime}}\}
, which satisfies our claim.

We may therefore assume that
P\subseteq H
and thus
P
has a trace
T
in
\rho^{\prime}
.
Since
P
is disjoint from the paths in
\mathcal{P}
, the trace of each path in
\mathcal{P}\setminus\{P_{p^{\prime}-1},P_{p^{\prime}}\}
in
\rho^{\prime}
splits
\Delta^{\prime}
into two disks, one of which contains
T
.
Let
T_{i}
be the trace of
P_{i}
for
i\in[p^{\prime}-2]
in
\rho^{\prime}
.
If
T
is found in the disk of
\Delta^{\prime}-T_{1}
that does not contain
T_{2}
, then
C_{E}
must intersect all paths in
\mathcal{P}
to reach
T
and we are done.
Analogously, if
T
is found in the disk of
\Delta^{\prime}-T_{p^{\prime}-3}
that does not contain
T_{1}
,
C_{W}
satisfies our requirements.
Thus, there instead exists an
i\in[p^{\prime}-3]
such that
T
is found in the intersection of the disk in
\Delta^{\prime}-T_{i}
that contains
T_{i+1}
and the disk in
\Delta^{\prime}-T_{i+1}
that contains
T_{i}
.
As a consequence, to reach this area, either
C_{W}
or
C_{E}
must intersect at least half of
\mathcal{P}\setminus\{P_{p^{\prime}}\}
, as required.

We may therefore move on to the case in which
\mathcal{W}
is a doublecross.
In this case,
\mathcal{W}
contains two pairs of paths
P,P^{\prime}
and
Q,Q^{\prime}
such that
P,P^{\prime}
have all of their endpoints on
C_{W}
and
Q,Q^{\prime}
have all of their endpoints on
C_{W}
, with both pairs forming crosses in the
C_{2}
-society in
\rho
.
Both
P
and
P^{\prime}
must intersect
C_{1}
and both
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
intersect all paths in
\mathcal{P}\setminus\{P_{p^{\prime}}\}
.
As a consequence, there exist four disjoint paths from
V(P\cup P^{\prime})
to
V(\Omega)
in
H
.
Thus there cannot exist a separation
(A,B)
of order at most three in
H
with
V(P\cup P^{\prime})\subseteq A
and
V(\Psi)\subseteq B
.
Due to
\rho^{\prime}
being a vortex-free rendition, this implies that
V(P\cup P^{\prime})\cap V(H)=\emptyset
.
Therefore
C_{W}
intersects all paths in
\mathcal{P}\setminus\{P_{p^{\prime}}\}
to escape
H
and reach
P
and
P^{\prime}
, which concludes the proof of our claim.

\blacksquare

Using
Claim

10.9.1
, we let
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
be a transaction of order
q\coloneqq 2p+5
such that
C_{E}
or
C_{W}
intersect all paths in
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
.
Solely for the purpose of simplifying notation, we assume that
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}=\{P_{1},\ldots,P_{q}\}
.
We remark that
\bigcup\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\subseteq H
.

Let

and let

.
Since
\mathcal{P}
is flat on one side and itself planar, flat, isolated, and separating,
(G,\Omega,\{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\})
is a
(13p+12,1)
-constriction of

.
In particular,

is a
(p+4,1)
-constriction of

with the frontier

such that

and

are disjoint.
Further, let

be the intersection of

and

, and note that

, which makes

a society.

Claim 10.9.2
.

There exists a
Z
-path

in

with

Proof of

:

.

be the

.

Note first that since

.

, this implies that there exists a

, as desired.

\blacksquare

Using
Claim

10.9.2
, we let
J
be a
Z
-path in
C_{2}
that intersects
P_{1}
and assume w.l.o.g. that

does not intersect

.
The trace
T_{J}
of

within
\rho_{L}
splits the disk

into two disks, of which we let
\Delta_{J}
be the disk that contains none of the vertices in

in the drawing associated with

.
Let
(U,\chi)
be the
\Delta_{J}
-society in
\rho_{L}
.
For each
i\in[12p+8]
, let

be the last vertex in

when traversing

starting from

.
Further, let

be

.
Furthermore, due to

.

Claim 10.9.3
.

Both
P_{i}^{\prime}
and
v_{i}P_{i}y_{i}^{\prime}
intersect
C_{1}
for all
i\in[3,12p+7]
and the

-path in

is internally disjoint from

.

Proof of
Claim

10.9.3
:

We have
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
, which is an exposed transaction in
\rho
since it has a crooked witness.
Thus each path in
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
contains an edge in
\sigma_{\rho}(c_{0})
and is thus exposed in
\rho
as well.
Recall that
\{C_{1},C_{2}\}
is a cozy nest in
\rho
.

We first consider
P_{2}^{\prime}
and
P_{12p+8}^{\prime}
, which both consist of components which are paths.
For each
i\in[2,12p+8]
, let
Q_{i}^{\prime}
be the last component of
P_{i}^{\prime}
, when traversing
P_{i}
starting from
x^{\prime}_{i}
, that contains at least one edge.
Further, let
z_{i}
be the endpoint of
Q_{i}^{\prime}
such that
x_{2}^{\prime}P_{i}z_{i}
contains
Q_{i}^{\prime}
.
Let
J^{\prime}
be the
z_{2}
-
z_{12p+8}
-path in
J
and note that
\{z_{i}~\!\colon\!~i\in[2,12p+8]\}\subseteq V(J)
, thanks to
\rho_{L}
being a vortex-free rendition of
(H^{\prime},\Psi^{\prime})
.

For all
i\in[3,12p+7]
, let
z_{i}^{\prime}
be the first vertex from
V(C_{2})
that is encountered when traversing
Q_{i}^{\prime}
starting from
z_{i}
.
Note that it is possible that
z_{i}^{\prime}\not\in V(J)
.
We let
Q_{i}^{\prime\prime}\coloneqq z_{i}^{\prime}Q_{i}^{\prime}z_{i}
for all
i\in[2,12+8]
and for the sake of simplicity, we let
R_{1}\coloneqq Q_{2}^{\prime\prime}
and
R_{2}\coloneqq Q_{12p+8}^{\prime\prime}
.

If both
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
intersect
C_{1}
, then let
Q^{\prime\prime}
be the unique
C_{1}
-path in
R_{1}\cup R_{2}\cup C_{2}
and note that for all
i\in[3,12p+7]
the trace of
Q_{i}^{\prime}
must pass through the trace of
Q^{\prime\prime}
to reach
z_{i}^{\prime}
if
Q_{i}^{\prime}
does not intersect
C_{2}
.
This immediately implies the existence of a
C_{2}
-path sticking out away from
c_{0}
in
\rho
, contradicting the coziness of
C_{2}
.

Thus at least one path in
\{R_{1},R_{2}\}
does not intersect
C_{1}
.
Suppose that for any
i\in[3,12p+7]
the path
Q_{i}^{\prime\prime}
does not intersect
C_{1}
.
Then we note that
z_{i}
and
z_{i}^{\prime}
separate
z_{2}
and
z_{12p+8}
on
C_{2}
.
In particular, there exists a
j\in[2]
, such that the
c_{0}
-disk of
C_{2}\cup Q_{i}^{\prime\prime}
does not contain the trace of
R_{i}
.
Furthermore, according to our assumptions on
C_{S}
not intersecting
J
, we know that
J^{\prime}
is disjoint from both
C_{S}
and
C_{N}
.
Thus, via
Lemma

10.8
, we arrive at a contradiction.

Using analogous methods, we can show that the paths
v_{i}P_{i}y_{i}^{\prime}
for
i\in[3,12p+8]
also behave as desired and allow us to confirm that the
C_{1}
-path in
P_{i}
is internally disjoint from
C_{2}-V(J)
.

\blacksquare

This claim now verifies that the following pair of vertices exists for each
i\in[3,12p+7]
.
Let
u_{i}
be the last vertex in
C_{1}\cap P_{i}^{\prime}
encountered when traversing
P_{i}
starting from
x_{i}^{\prime}
and let
w_{i}
be the first vertex of
C_{1}\cap P_{i}
encountered when traversing
v_{i}P_{i}y_{i}^{\prime}
starting from
v_{i}
.
To aid intuition, we note that, when traversing
P_{i}
from
x_{i}^{\prime}
to
y_{i}^{\prime}
, the vertices
u_{i},v_{i},w_{i}
are encountered in the given order and in particular,
P_{i}^{*}\coloneqq u_{i}P_{i}w_{i}
is both internally disjoint from
C_{1}
and entirely disjoint from
C_{2}-V(J)
.

Let
A\coloneqq\{u_{i}~\!\colon\!~i\in[3,12p+7]\}
and
B\coloneqq\{w_{i}~\!\colon\!~i\in[3,12p+7]\}
.
We will now see that
A
and
B
– which are both found in
V(C_{1})\cap V(U)
– are in fact highly connected within
C_{1}
.

Claim 10.9.4
.

There exists an
A
-
B
-linkage in
C_{1}
of order
12p+5
.

Proof of
Claim

10.9.4
:

We start by noting that
|A|=|B|=p-1
and thus, if the claimed linkage exists, it connects every vertex in
A
to a vertex in
B
and vice versa.
In fact, as we will show, for each
i\in[3,12p+7]
there exists a
u_{i}
-
w_{i}
-path
Q_{i}\subseteq C_{1}
that is disjoint from
(A\cup B)\setminus\{u_{i},w_{i}\}
.

Let
i\in[3,12p+7]
be arbitrary.
The path
P_{i}^{*}
has the endpoints
u_{i}
and
w_{i}
, intersects
C_{2}
, and is internally disjoint from
C_{1}
.
Thus
P_{i}^{*}
must be found in the union
G_{1}
of
C_{1}
and the outer graph of
C_{1}
in
\rho
.
Furthermore, due to the coziness of
\mathcal{C}
and the fact that
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
is orthogonal to
C_{3}
,
P_{i}^{*}
must also be found in the union of
C_{2}
and the inner graph of
C_{2}
.
We can split
C_{1}
into two internally disjoint
u_{i}
-
w_{i}
-paths
R_{i}
and
R_{i}^{\prime}
for each
i\in[3,p+1]
and we choose to label these paths such that the trace of
R_{i}\cup P_{i}^{*}
does not separate
N_{\rho}(\Omega)
from
c_{0}
in
\rho
, which must be true for exactly one of the two paths
R_{i}
and
R_{i}^{\prime}
.

Suppose that there exist distinct
i,j\in[3,12p+7]
such that
R_{i}
and
R_{j}
intersect.
Note that
u_{i},u_{j},w_{i},w_{j}
are four distinct vertices.
Thus
R_{i}
must contain an endpoint of
R_{j}
in its interior.
W.l.o.g. we may thus assume that
u_{j}\in V(R_{i})\setminus\{u_{i},w_{i}\}
.

Should
w_{j}\not\in V(R_{i})
, we note that
V(P_{i}^{*})
separates
u_{j}
from
w_{j}
in
G_{1}
, since
\rho
is a cylindrical rendition.
This contradicts the fact that
P_{i}^{*}
and
P_{j}^{*}
are disjoint.

We may therefore further assume that
w_{j}\in V(R_{i})
in addition to
u_{j}\in V(R_{i})
.
However, in this case,
P_{i}^{*}
separates
C_{2}-V(P_{i}^{*})
from
u_{j}
and
w_{j}
in
G_{1}
.
This in turn contradicts the fact that
v_{j}\in V(P_{j}^{*})\cap V(C_{2})
.
Therefore, our desired
A
-
B
-linkage is in fact
\{R_{i}~\!\colon\!~i\in[3,12p+7]\}
.

\blacksquare

Before we discuss the linkage this claim yields for us, we turn our attention back to
\rho_{\mathfrak{N}}
,
J
,
U
, and
G^{*}
, which were defined above.
We apply
Lemma

9.18
to ask for a crooked transaction of order
p
in
(G^{*},\Omega^{\prime})
.
If this returns a crooked transaction, this lets us apply
Lemma

9.16
within
(G^{*},\Omega^{\prime})
to find a crooked transaction
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
of order
p
with all of its endpoints in
V(\Omega^{\prime})\cap V(\Omega)
.
Thus we have found a crooked transaction of order
p
in
(G,\Omega)

In particular, due to the fact that we found the linkage constituting the crooked transaction in
G^{*}
, we know that
\bigcup\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}\subseteq\bigcup\mathcal{P}\cup C_{1}\cup C_{2}
, as desired.
It turns out that our application of
Lemma

9.16
is in fact the most computationally costly aspect of our proof.

Suppose that
Lemma

9.16
instead returns a cylindrical rendition
\rho^{*}
of
(G^{*},\Omega^{\prime})
of depth at most
12p+4
.
Let
\mathcal{L}
be the
A
-
B
-linkage of order
12p+5
, whose paths are all contained in
C_{1}
, that we found in
Claim

10.9.4
.
The existence of
\mathcal{L}
begs a simple question.
How exactly is
\mathcal{L}
drawn within
\rho_{\mathfrak{N}}
, whilst
\mathcal{L}
and
C_{2}
are disjoint?

Let
A^{\prime}
be the last vertices of
V(U)
that the paths in
\mathcal{L}
see when starting from
A
and note that
A^{\prime}\subseteq Z
.
Similarly, let
B^{\prime}
be the first vertices of
H^{\prime}-V(U)
that the paths in
\mathcal{L}
see when traversing them from
A^{\prime}
to
B
.
We again note that
B^{\prime}\subseteq Z
.
Using this perspective, it is easy to see that
\bigcup\mathcal{L}\cap G^{*}
contains an
A^{\prime}
-
B^{\prime}
-linkage
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
of order
12p+5
.
Since
A^{\prime}\cup B^{\prime}\subseteq Z\subseteq V(\Omega^{\prime})
, the linkage
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
is also a transaction in
(G^{*},\Omega^{\prime})
.

The definition of
(H^{\prime},\Psi^{\prime})
and the associated vortex-free rendition
\rho_{L}
allows us to combine
\rho^{*}
with
\rho_{L}
into a cylindrical rendition
\tau
of
(H^{*},\Omega)
, where
H^{*}=H^{\prime}\cup G^{*}\subseteq G
, around a single vortex
c_{1}
in
\Delta
.
We note that
\bigcup\mathcal{L}\subseteq H^{*}
,
A\cup B\subseteq V(H^{*})
, and
C_{2}\subseteq H^{*}
.
Suppose that
C_{2}
is grounded in
\tau
, then the fact that
A
and
B
are separated by
V(J)
in
H^{\prime}
in fact extends to
A
and
B
being separated by
V(C_{2})
in
H^{*}
, since
C_{2}
is a cycle.
This is a clear contradiction to the existence of
\mathcal{L}
, which is disjoint from
V(C_{2})
.
Thus
C_{2}
has some edge that is drawn in
\sigma_{\tau}(c_{1})
.

As a consequence of this,
J
is contained in a
\sigma_{\tau}(c_{1})
-path
J^{\prime}
within
C_{2}
.
Note that thanks to
Claim

10.9.3
we know that for all
i\in[12p+5]
the path
P_{i}^{*}
is disjoint from
C_{2}-V(J)
and thus in particular from
J^{\prime}-V(J)
.
Since
\rho_{L}
is a restriction of
\tau
, this implies that
J^{\prime}
separates
A
from
B
in
H^{*}-(V(\sigma_{\tau}(c_{1}))\setminus\pi_{\tau}(\widetilde{c_{1}}))
.
As
J^{\prime}
meets the boundary of
c_{1}
in at least two nodes of
\tau
, we note that this fact implies that each path of
\mathcal{L}
must also meet the boundary of
c_{1}
in
\tau
.
Hence
J^{\prime}
is grounded in
\tau
.
We may thus let
T^{\prime}
be the trace of
J^{\prime}
.
Within
\Delta-(T^{\prime}\cup c_{1})
there exists a unique disk
\Delta_{J^{\prime}}
whose closure contains
U
and in particular
A
in the drawing provided by
\tau
.
We let
U^{\prime}
be the crop of
H^{*}
to
\Delta_{J^{\prime}}
in
\tau
.

This allows us to take
S=U^{\prime}\cap\pi_{\tau}(\widetilde{c_{1}})
, which defines a segment of the vortex-society
(G_{c_{1}},\Omega_{c_{1}})
of
c_{1}
in
\tau
.
Each path in
\mathcal{L}
must also meet
V(\Omega_{c_{1}}\setminus S)
.
Therefore there exists a transaction of order
12p+5
in
(G_{c_{1}},\Omega_{c_{1}})
with one of its end segments contained in
S
and the other in
V(\Omega_{c_{1}})\setminus S
.
This clearly contradicts the depth of the cylindrical rendition
\rho^{*}
, which is a restriction of
\tau
, and thus we cannot find the rendition
\rho^{*}
and must instead have found a crooked transaction when applying
Lemma

9.18
earlier.
∎

We can now apply this lemma to prove that the presence of a crooked witness for a transaction suffices to allow us to find an orthogonal crooked transaction, with some loss in the size of the desired transaction.

Lemma 10.10
.

Let the function
\mathsf{f}_{\ref{lem:orthogonalexposedapply}}\colon\mathbb{N}^{2}\mapsto\mathbb{N}
be defined such that
\mathsf{f}_{\ref{lem:orthogonalexposedapply}}(x,y)=56xy-56x+52y-59
for all positive integers
x,y
.

Let
p,s
be integers with
p\geq 4
and
s\geq 3
and let
(G,\Omega)
be a society with a cylindrical rendition
\rho
with a cozy nest
\mathcal{C}=\{C_{1},\ldots,C_{s}\}
of order
s
around a vortex
c_{0}
in the disk
\Delta
.
Further, let
\mathcal{Q}
be a transaction of order
\mathsf{f}_{\ref{lem:orthogonalexposedapply}}(p,s)
in
(G,\Omega)
with a crooked witness
\mathcal{W}
on
C_{2}
.

Then there exists a crooked transaction
\mathcal{P}
of order
p
in
(G,\Omega)
orthogonal to
\{C_{3},\ldots,C_{s}\}
.

Moreover, there exists an algorithm that finds
\mathcal{P}
in time
\mathbf{O}(ps|E(G)||V(G)|^{2})
.

Proof.

We refine
\mathcal{Q}
by applying
Lemma

10.7
to
\mathcal{Q}
and
\mathcal{W}
.
Since
56ps-56p+52s-59=(56p+52)(s-2)+(56p+45)
, this yields a transaction
\mathcal{P}_{1}
of order
56p+45
such that
\mathcal{W}
is a crooked witness for
\mathcal{P}_{1}
on
C_{2}
and
\mathcal{P}_{1}
is orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\coloneqq\{C_{3},C_{4},\ldots,C_{s}\}
.
At this point an application of
Proposition

9.2
to
\mathcal{P}_{1}
either yields a crooked transaction of order
p
that is orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
– in which case we are done – , or we find a planar transaction
\mathcal{P}_{2}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{1}
of order
55p+47
, since
\mathcal{P}_{1}
has order
(55p+47)+p-2
.
Note that accordingly
\mathcal{P}_{2}
is still orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{W}
remains a crooked witness for
\mathcal{P}_{2}
on
C_{2}
.

Now, we apply
Proposition

9.9
to
\mathcal{P}_{2}
.
Should this yield a crooked transaction
\mathcal{P}_{3}
of order
p
, then this transaction is orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
, since
\mathcal{P}_{3}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{1}
, as desired in the second point of our statement.
We may thus assume that we instead find a planar, flat, isolated, separating transaction
\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{2}
of order
p^{\prime}\coloneqq 26p+25
that is flat on one side, since
\mathcal{P}_{2}
has order
55p+47=3p+52p+47
.

Let
\mathcal{P}=\{P_{1},\ldots,P_{p^{\prime}}\}
be indexed naturally.
As a consequence of
\mathcal{P}
being flat on one side, we may assume that the labels of the paths in
\mathcal{P}
are chosen such that the following society
(H,\Psi)
has a vortex-free rendition
\rho^{\prime}
into a disk
\Delta^{\prime}
.
Let
I
be the unique segment of
\Omega
that has the same endpoints as
P_{p^{\prime}-1}
and does not contain the endpoints of
P_{p^{\prime}}
.
Further, let
H^{*}
be the graph consisting of
\mathcal{P}\setminus\{P_{p^{\prime}}\}
and the vertices in
I
, with
H
being the union of
H^{*}
and all
H^{*}
-bridges in
G
with at least one attachment in
V(H^{*})\setminus V(P_{p^{\prime}-1})
.
As noted in
Definition

9.8
,
H
must be disjoint from both
P_{p^{\prime}-1}
and the vertices in
V(\Omega)\setminus I
.
The society
(H,\Psi)
that we mentioned above is then defined by choosing
\Psi
such that
V(\Psi)=I\cup(\pi(N(\rho))\cap V(P_{p^{\prime}-1}))
, with the order on
I
being obtained naturally from
\Omega
and the order of
\pi(N(\rho))\cap V(P_{p^{\prime}-1})
being derived from a traversal of
P_{p^{\prime}-1}
.

For each
i\in[p^{\prime}]
let
x_{i},y_{i}
be the two distinct endpoints of
P_{i}
, let
x_{i}^{\prime}
be the last vertex in
\pi(N(\rho))\cap V(C_{3}\cap P_{i})
that is found when traversing
P_{i}
from
x_{i}
before encountering a vertex in
V(\sigma(c_{0}))
, and let
y_{i}^{\prime}
be defined analogously.
Let
X^{\prime}=\{x_{i}^{\prime}~\!\colon\!~i\in[p^{\prime}]\}
, let
Y^{\prime}=\{y_{i}^{\prime}~\!\colon\!~i\in[p^{\prime}]\}
, let
(G_{3},\Omega_{3})
be the
C_{3}
-society in
\rho
, and note that
X^{\prime}\cup Y^{\prime}\subseteq V(\Omega_{3})
.
We let
\Omega^{\prime}
be a cyclic ordering of
X^{\prime}\cup Y^{\prime}
derived from the ordering of these vertices in
\Omega_{3}
.
Thus both
X^{\prime}
and
Y^{\prime}
correspond to segments of
\Omega^{\prime}
.
Further, let

G^{\prime}\coloneq(G_{3}-(\bigcup_{i=1}^{q}(x_{i}P_{i}x_{i}^{\prime}\cup y_{i}P_{i}y_{i}^{\prime})-(X^{\prime}\cup Y^{\prime}))).

By definition
(G^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime})
is a society.
For each
i\in[p^{\prime}]
, we let
P_{i}^{\prime}=x_{i}^{\prime}P_{i}y_{i}^{\prime}
and we let
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}=\{P_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,P_{p^{\prime}}^{\prime}\}
.
Since
\mathcal{P}
is orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
, we have
\bigcup\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\subseteq G^{\prime}
.
The transaction
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
in fact inherits many properties of
\mathcal{P}
.

Claim 10.10.1
.

\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
is planar, flat, isolated, separating, and flat on one side in
(G^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime})
.

Proof of
Claim

10.10.1
:

Let
(G_{P},\Omega_{P})
be the
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
-strip in
(G^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime})
.
The fact that
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
is planar is immediate from its definition.
We note that due to
\bigcup\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\subseteq\bigcup\mathcal{P}
and the fact that
(H,\Psi)
has a vortex-free rendition, we know that
G_{P}\subseteq H
.
Should
(G_{P},\Omega_{P})
contain a cross consisting of two paths
P,P^{\prime}
, these paths must therefore also be found in
H
.
Due to our choice of
X\cup Y
, this pair of paths can be extended to a cross in
(H,\Psi)
, a contradiction.
Thus, according to
Proposition

3.1
,
(G_{P},\Omega_{P})
has a vortex-free rendition, which means that
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
is flat.
The facts that
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
is isolated, separating, and flat on one side are also consequences of
(H,\Psi)
having a vortex-free rendition via analogous arguments.

\blacksquare

This now allows us to apply
Lemma

10.9
to
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
to find a crooked transaction
\mathcal{P}^{\prime\prime}
of order
p
whose endpoints lie in
X^{\prime}\cup Y^{\prime}
.
Therefore, once we route back
\mathcal{P}^{\prime\prime}
onto vertices in
V(\Omega)
via the infrastructure in
\bigcup\mathcal{P}\setminus V(G^{\prime})
, we have found a crooked transaction
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime\prime}
of order
p
on
(G,\Omega)
.
By construction
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime\prime}
is orthogonal to
\{C_{4},\ldots,C_{s}\}
.
In addition, as the vertices in
V(C_{3}\cap G^{*})
are all of degree 1 in
G^{*}
, if such a vertex is contained in
\bigcup\mathcal{Q}^{\prime\prime}
, they must be an endpoint of a path in
\mathcal{P}^{\prime\prime}
.
As a consequence
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime\prime}
is orthogonal to
\{C_{3},\ldots,C_{s}\}
.
This completes our proof.
∎

We can now apply this lemma to prove the main theorem of this section, which can be seen as a strengthening of
Lemma

9.18
.
As mentioned earlier, we do not find an explicit application for this theorem in our later proofs and instead rely on
Lemma

10.10
.

Theorem 10.11
.

Let
p,s
be integers with
p\geq 4
and
s\geq 3
and let
(G,\Omega)
be a society with a cylindrical rendition
\rho
with a cozy nest
\mathcal{C}=\{C_{1},\ldots,C_{s}\}
around a vortex
c_{0}
in the disk
\Delta
.

Then one of the following holds:

i)

(G,\Omega)
has a cylindrical rendition
\rho^{\prime}
in
\Delta
of depth at most
2016ps-2016p+1968s-2288
, or

ii)

there exists a crooked transaction
\mathcal{P}
of order
p
in
(G,\Omega)
orthogonal to
\{C_{3},\ldots,C_{s}\}
.

Moreover, there exists an algorithm that finds
\rho^{\prime}
or
\mathcal{P}
in time
\mathbf{O}(ps|E(G)||V(G)|^{2})
.

Proof.

We start by asking
Lemma

9.18
for a crooked transaction of order
168ps-168p+164s-191
in
(G,\Omega)
.
If this yields a cylindrical rendition of
(G,\Omega)
with depth at most

\mathsf{f}_{\ref{thm:orthogonalexposed}}(p,s)=2016ps-2016p+1968s-2288=12(168ps-168p+164s-191)+4,

we are done.
Thus we can assume that
Lemma

9.18
yields a crooked transaction
\mathcal{P}_{1}
of order

168ps-168p+164s-191=8s-14+3((56p+52)(s-2)+(56p+45)).

By applying
Lemma

10.5
, we find a transaction
\mathcal{P}_{2}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{1}
of order
(56p+52)(s-2)+(56p+45)=56sp-56p+52s-59
, together with a crooked witness
\mathcal{W}
on
C_{2}
.
This in turn allows us to apply
Lemma

10.10
, to find the crooked transaction
\mathcal{P}
we ask for in the statement.
∎

11
Orthogonalisation with respect to several societies

As we will see in
Section

12
, we actually need
Theorem

10.11
from
Section

10
to be applicable in a setting in which we have multiple societies contained in one another.
Let us first define what we mean by this.

Let
s,k
be a positive integers.
Let
(G_{0},\Omega_{0})
be a society and let
\{(G_{i},\Omega_{i})\}_{i\in[k]}
be a family of
k
societies, such that for all
i\in[k]
the society
(G_{i},\Omega_{i})
has a cylindrical rendition
\rho_{i}
in a disk
\Delta_{i}
with a cozy nest
\mathcal{C}_{i}
of order
s
around a vortex
c_{i}
such that
V(\Omega_{0})\cup V(\bigcup_{j\in[k]\setminus\{j\}}G_{j}-(\sigma_{\rho_{j}}(c_{j})-\widetilde{c_{j}})\subseteq G_{i}-(\sigma_{\rho_{i}}(c_{i})-\widetilde{c_{i}})
.
Furthermore, let it be true that
G_{i}\subseteq G_{0}
for all
i\in[k]
.
If these conditions are met, we call
\{((G_{i},\Omega_{i}),\rho_{i},c_{i},\Delta_{i},\mathcal{C}_{i})\}_{i\in[k]}
a
partial
(s,k)
-society configuration in
(G_{0},\Omega_{0})
.

Should there further exist a cylindrical rendition
\rho_{0}
of
(G_{0},\Omega_{0})
in a disk
\Delta_{0}
with a nest
\mathcal{C}_{0}
of order
s
around a vortex
c_{0}
, such that
V(\bigcup_{i\in[k]}G_{i}-(\sigma_{\rho_{i}}(c_{i})-\widetilde{c_{i}})\subseteq G_{0}-(\sigma_{\rho_{0}}(c_{0})-\widetilde{c_{0}})
, we call
\{((G_{i},\Omega_{i}),\rho_{i},c_{i},\Delta_{i},\mathcal{C}_{i})\}_{i\in[0,k]}
a
full
(s,k)
-society configuration
.

We note that, letting
H_{i}^{\prime}
be the union of
C_{1}^{i}
and the outer graph of
C_{1}^{i}
in
\rho_{i}
, the graphs
H_{i}^{\prime}
and
H_{j}^{\prime}
are disjoint for all distinct
i,j\in[k]
, if we are dealing with a partial configuration, and for all distinct
i,j\in[0,k]
in a full configuration.

This now allows us to define a new type of crooked witness.

Definition 11.1

(Co-conspirators)
.

Let
s,k,h
be a positive integers with
s\geq h
.
Let
\{((G_{i},\Omega_{i}),\rho_{i},c_{i},\Delta_{i},\mathcal{C}_{i})\}_{i\in[k]}
be a partial
(s,k)
-society configuration in the society
(G_{0},\Omega_{0})
.
Further, let
\mathcal{P}
be a transaction in
(G_{0},\Omega_{0})
.
Let
X,Y
be the end segments of
\mathcal{P}
, with
A,B
being the two distinct segments of
\Omega_{0}
that have one endpoint in
X
and the other in
Y
.

Let
Q
be an
A
-
V(C_{h}^{i})
-path with
i\in[k]
in
G_{0}-V(\bigcup\mathcal{P})
that is internally disjoint from
V(\Omega_{0})
.
If there exists a pair of
B
-paths
P,P^{\prime}
in
G_{0}-V(\bigcup\mathcal{P}\cup Q)
that form a cross in
(G_{0},\Omega_{0})
, we call
\{Q,P,P^{\prime}\}

turncoats (for
\mathcal{P}
in
(G_{0},\Omega_{0})
on level
h
to
i
)
.
Else, if there exists a
B
-
V(C_{h}^{j})
-path
Q^{\prime}
with
j\in[k]
in
G_{0}-V(\bigcup\mathcal{P})
that is internally disjoint from
V(\Omega_{0})\cup V(Q)
, we call
\{Q,Q^{\prime}\}

deserters (for
\mathcal{P}
in
(G_{0},\Omega_{0})
on level
h
to
i
and
j
)
.

A set of paths
\mathcal{W}
in
G_{0}
are
co-conspirators (for
\mathcal{P}
in
(G_{0},\Omega_{0})
on level
h
)
if
\mathcal{W}
is a crooked witness for
\mathcal{P}
in
(G_{0},\Omega_{0})
on
(G_{0},\Omega_{0})
, or
\mathcal{W}
are turncoats or deserters for
\mathcal{P}
in
(G_{0},\Omega_{0})
(on level
h
).

The following is another surprisingly easy variant of
Lemma

10.6
that deals with co-conspirators, similar to how
Lemma

10.7
preserves the existence of a crooked witness.

Lemma 11.2
.

Let
s,p,k
be positive integers with
s\geq 3
.
Let
\{((G_{i},\Omega_{i}),\rho_{i},c_{i},\Delta_{i},\mathcal{C}_{i}=\{C_{1}^{i},\ldots,C_{s}^{i}\})\}_{i\in[k]}
be a partial
(s,k)
-society configuration in the society
(G_{0},\Omega_{0})
.
Let
\mathcal{P}
be a transaction of order
(p+1)(s-2)+p
in
(G_{0},\Omega_{0})
with the end segments
X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}
, let
X
be the set of endpoints of paths in
\mathcal{P}
found in
X^{\prime}
, and let
Y
be defined analogously for
Y^{\prime}
.
Further, let
\mathcal{W}
be co-conspirators for
\mathcal{P}
in
(G_{0},\Omega_{0})
(on level 2) such that the paths in
\mathcal{W}
are internally disjoint from
V(C_{2}^{i})
for all
i\in[k]
.

Then there exists a transaction
\mathcal{Q}
in
(G_{0},\Omega_{0})
that is an
X
-
Y
-linkage of order
p
and
\bigcup\mathcal{Q}
is disjoint from both
\bigcup\mathcal{W}
and the union of
\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}(H_{i}^{2}-V(C_{2}^{i}))
, where
H_{i}^{2}
is the outer graph of
C_{2}^{i}
in
\rho_{i}
.

In particular,
\mathcal{Q}
can be found in time
\mathbf{O}(p|E(G)|)
.

Proof sketch.

Let
H_{i}=H_{i}^{2}-V(C_{2}^{i})
for each
i\in[k]
and let
G=G_{0}-\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}V(H_{i})
.
We orthogonalise two radial linkages leading to
X
and
Y
respectively as in the proof of
Lemma

10.6
and apply
Menger’s theorem
within
G
to ask for an
X
-
Y
-linkage of order
p
.
Of course if this yields a linkage, we are immediately done and have justified the runtime bound we claim in our statement.

We may therefore instead assume that we find an
X
-
Y
-separator
S
of order at most
p-1
in
G
.
First let
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
be a transaction of order at least
(p+1)(s-2)+1
whose paths avoid
S
.
Further, let
Z=V(\bigcup\mathcal{W})\cap\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}V(C_{2}^{i})
be the set of endpoints the paths in
\mathcal{W}
have in
\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}V(C_{2}^{i})
.
We note that
|Z|\leq 2
, since the paths in
\mathcal{W}
are internally disjoint from
V(C_{i}^{2})
for all
i\in[0,k]
, with the worst-case being assumed by a pair of deserters.

Let
i\in[k]
be arbitrary.
If
F
is a
C_{2}^{i}
-path in
G_{0}
that sticks out away from
c_{i}
in
\rho_{i}
, we let
F^{\prime}
be the unique subpath of
C_{1}
such that
F^{\prime}
has the same endpoints as
F
and
F\cup F^{\prime}
is the unique cycle whose trace in
\rho_{i}
does not separate
c_{i}
and
\Omega_{i}
.
As in the proof of
Lemma

10.6
, we call
F^{\prime}
the
gangplank
of
F
.
Let
F^{\prime\prime}
be the path in
C_{2}^{i}
with the same endpoints as
F
that is distinct from
F^{\prime}
.
We call
F^{\prime\prime}
the
gangboard
of
F
that lies
opposite to the gangplank
F^{\prime}
.
A subpath
F^{\prime}
of
C_{2}^{i}
that is a gangplank for some subpath of a path
\widetilde{P}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}
is also called a
gangplank
of
\widetilde{P}
and analogously, if
F^{\prime}
was a gangboard, it would be called a
gangboard
of
\widetilde{P}
.

Let
S^{\prime}=(S\cap\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}V(C_{2}^{i}))\cup Z
and note that
|S^{\prime}|\leq p+1
.
Suppose there exists some path
\widetilde{P}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}
such that whenever a gangplank of
\widetilde{P}
intersects
S^{\prime}
, the gangboard opposite to it is disjoint from
S^{\prime}
.
Then the union of
\widetilde{P}\cap G
and all of its gangplanks and gangboards that are disjoint from
S^{\prime}
forms a connected subgraph containing a vertex of
X
and a vertex of
Y
.
This contradicts the fact that
S
is an
X
-
Y
-separator and thus no such path
\widetilde{P}
may exist.

Clearly, no path in
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}
can be found in
G-S
and thus, every path in
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}
has a gangplank.
In particular, thanks to our prior observation, we conclude that every path in
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}
has a gangplank that is intersected by
S^{\prime}
and for which the gangboard opposing it is also intersected by
S^{\prime}
.
As we have
|\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}|\geq(p+1)(s-2)+1
and
|S^{\prime}|\leq p+1
, this means that there exists some
u\in S^{\prime}
that is contained in the gangplank of
s-1
paths
\widehat{P}_{1},\dots,\widehat{P}_{s-1}\in\widehat{\mathcal{P}}
, with all of these gangplanks being contained in
C_{2}^{i}
for some fixed
i\in[k]
.
From this point we reach a contradiction via entirely analogous methods to those presented in the proof of
Lemma

10.6
and thus we omit this part.
∎

We will also need a variant of
Lemma

10.8
.
In our current context, the variant we need is actually easier to state than the original and the proof is analogous.
We thus present the following lemma without proof.

Lemma 11.3
.

Let
p
be an integer with
p\geq 2
.
Let
(G,\Omega)
be a society with a cylindrical rendition
\rho
around a vortex
c_{0}
with a cozy nest
\mathcal{C}=\{C_{1},C_{2}\}
.
Further, let
\mathcal{P}
be a linkage in
G
with its endpoints outside of the union
H_{1}
of
C_{1}
and the outer graph of
C_{1}
in
\rho
.

Then for any pair of distinct paths
R,Q\in\mathcal{P}
and any two
C_{2}
-subpaths
R^{\prime}\subseteq R
and
Q^{\prime}\subseteq Q
found in
H_{1}
, the
c_{0}
-disk of
C_{2}\cup R^{\prime}
contains the trace of
Q^{\prime}
in
\rho
and vice versa.

We can now prove the main theorem, a variant of
Theorem

10.11
, which we split into two parts.
The first of which, a lemma which we will be able to reuse in the next section, contains the main arguments, though it requires a rather specific setup to apply.
Our overall proof has many similarities to the proof of
Theorem

10.11
, though it is also made more technical by the addition of another variable, which represents the number of societies we are dealing with.

The following lemma may seem extremely strange, since it all it seemingly does is replace co-conspirators on level 2 with co-conspirators on level 3 at some cost to the size of the transaction.
But this is in fact a massive difference for us.
As we will see in the proof, the fact that our transaction is disjoint from
C_{3}^{i}
means that, if we have co-conspirators on level 3, we can actually say something meaningful about the location of the society in whose nests
C_{3}^{i}
is found.
On the other hand the witness on level 2 tells us basically nothing about the location of this society.

Lemma 11.4
.

Let
p,s,k
be non-negative integers with
p\geq 4
and
s\geq 3
.
Let
\{((G_{i},\Omega_{i}),\rho_{i},c_{i},\Delta_{i},\mathcal{C}_{i}=\{C_{1}^{i},\ldots,C_{s}^{i}\})\}_{i\in[0,k]}
be a full
(s,k)
-society configuration.
Further, let
\mathcal{Q}
be a transaction in
(G_{0},\Omega_{0})
of order
130kp+3p-80k+9
that is disjoint from the union of
C_{3}^{i}
and the outer graph
G_{3}^{i}
of
C_{3}^{i}
in
\rho_{i}
for all
i\in[k]
and such that
\mathcal{Q}
has co-conspirators
\mathcal{W}
(on level 2).
Finally, assume that
(G_{0},\Omega_{0})
contains a crooked transaction of order 53.

We can now finally refine our results from

10
to respect the presence of several other nests and societies.
Note that with only a small amount of effort we could extend the below result to a full analogue of

.
We chose not to do this since the result is cumbersome to state – like the other results of this section – and is not needed for our arguments.

Theorem 11.5
.

Let the function
\mathsf{f}_{\ref{thm:orthogonalexposedsorcery}}\colon\mathbb{N}^{3}\mapsto\mathbb{N}
be defined such that
\mathsf{f}_{\ref{thm:orthogonalexposedsorcery}}(x,y,z)=650xyz-650xz-400yz+400z+15xy-15x+55y-32
for all positive integers
x,y,z
.

Let
p,s,k
be non-negative integers with
p\geq 4
and
s\geq 3
.
Let
\{((G_{i},\Omega_{i}),\rho_{i},c_{i},\Delta_{i},\mathcal{C}_{i}=\{C_{1}^{i},\ldots,C_{s}^{i}\})\}_{i\in[0,k]}
be a full
(s,k)
-society configuration.
Further, let
\mathcal{P}_{1}
be a crooked transaction in
(G_{0},\Omega_{0})
of order
\mathsf{f}_{\ref{thm:orthogonalexposedsorcery}}(p,s,k)
orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\coloneqq\{C_{3}^{0},\ldots,C_{s}^{0}\}
.

Then there exists a transaction
\mathcal{P}
of order
p
with co-conspirators
\mathcal{W}
in
(G_{0},\Omega_{0})
(on level 3) that is orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
, and disjoint from the union of
C_{3}^{i}
and the outer graphs
C_{3}^{i}
in
\rho_{i}
for all
i\in[k]
.

Moreover, there exists an algorithm that finds
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{W}
in time
\mathbf{O}(psk|E(G)||V(G)|^{2})
.

Proof.

Let
q\coloneqq 130kp+3p-80k+9
.
We can use
Lemma

9.4
to find a crooked transaction
\mathcal{P}_{2}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{1}
that is a leap of order
(q+2)(s-2)+q+9
or a doublecross of order
(q+2)(s-2)+q+9
, which is possible since
\mathcal{P}_{1}
has order
\mathsf{f}_{\ref{thm:orthogonalexposedsorcery}}(p,s,k)=5qs-5q+10s+13=4((q+2)(s-2)+q+9-2)+(q+2)(s-2)+q+9-4
.
Since
\mathcal{P}_{2}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{1}
, it is in particular orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
.

Let
X,Y
be the two end segments of
\mathcal{P}_{2}
in
\Omega_{0}
and let
X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}
be the set of endpoints contained in
X
and
Y
respectively.
For each
P\in\mathcal{P}_{2}
, we let
x_{P}^{\prime}\in X^{\prime}
and
y_{P}^{\prime}\in Y^{\prime}
be its endpoints.
Further, we let
x_{P}
be the last vertex of
\pi(N(\rho))\cap V(C_{3}^{0})
that is found on
P\in\mathcal{P}_{2}
when traversing it from
x_{P}
until a vertex of
V(\sigma(c_{0}))
is encountered and let
y_{P}
be defined analogously.
We let
X^{\prime\prime}\coloneqq\{x_{P}~\!\colon\!~P\in\mathcal{P}_{2}\}
and
Y^{\prime\prime}\coloneqq\{y_{P}~\!\colon\!~P\in\mathcal{P}_{2}\}
.
Let
(G_{3},\Omega_{3})
be the
C_{3}
-society in
\rho_{0}
and note that
X^{\prime\prime}\cup Y^{\prime\prime}\subseteq V(\Omega_{3})
.
We define the ordering
\Omega^{\prime}
with
V(\Omega^{\prime})=X^{\prime\prime}\cup Y^{\prime\prime}
by taking the cyclic ordering induced by
\Omega_{3}
on
V(\Omega^{\prime})
, according to which
X^{\prime\prime}
and
Y^{\prime\prime}
each correspond to segments of
\Omega^{\prime}
.
Further, let

G^{\prime}\coloneqq G_{3}-(\bigcup_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{2}}(x_{P}^{\prime}Px_{P}\cup y_{P}^{\prime}Py_{P})-(X^{\prime\prime}\cup Y^{\prime\prime})).

By definition
(G^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime})
is a society.
We let
\mathcal{P}_{3}\coloneqq\{x_{P}Py_{P}~\!\colon\!~P\in\mathcal{P}_{2}\}
and note that
\mathcal{P}_{3}
is a crooked transaction on
(G^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime})
of the same order as
\mathcal{P}_{3}
, since
\mathcal{P}_{3}
is orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
.
In particular, if
\mathcal{P}_{2}
is a leap then
\mathcal{P}_{3}
is a leap and similarly, if
\mathcal{P}_{2}
is a doublecross then
\mathcal{P}_{3}
is also a doublecross.
We note that this certifies that
(G^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime})
contains a crooked transaction of order at least
53
, since
p\geq 4
and
s\geq 3
.
This fact will be required later for us to apply
Lemma

11.4
.

If
\mathcal{P}_{3}
is a leap, let
P\in\mathcal{P}_{3}
be the overpass in
\mathcal{P}_{3}
(see
Section

9.2
).
Should
P
be disjoint from the union of
C_{2}^{i}
for all
i\in[k]
, we let
\mathcal{W}=\{P\}
.
Otherwise, let
R_{x}
be the unique
x_{P}
-
V(\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}C_{2}^{i})
-path in
P
, let
R_{y}
be the unique
y_{P}
-
V(\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}C_{2}^{i})
-path in
P
, and let
\mathcal{W}=\{R_{x},R_{y}\}
.
On the other hand, if
\mathcal{P}_{3}
is a doublecross, then we let
P,P^{\prime},Q,Q^{\prime}
be the four paths in
\mathcal{P}_{3}
that form a doublecross for
\mathcal{P}_{3}\setminus\{P,P^{\prime},Q,Q^{\prime}\}
such that
P,P^{\prime}
and
Q,Q^{\prime}
each form a cross in
(G^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime})
.
If
P\cup P^{\prime}
is disjoint from
C_{2}^{i}
for all
i\in[k]
, we add
P,P^{\prime}
to
\mathcal{W}
and otherwise, we let
R_{P}
be a
(V(P\cup P^{\prime})\cap V(\Omega^{\prime}))
-
V(\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}C_{2}^{i})
-path in
P\cup P^{\prime}
and add
R_{P}
to
\mathcal{W}
.
We proceed analogously with
Q\cup Q^{\prime}
, which results in
\mathcal{W}
being a doublecross, pair of turncoats, or pair of deserters for
\mathcal{P}_{3}
.

Independent of whether
\mathcal{P}_{3}
is a leap or a doublecross, we can thus find a transaction
\mathcal{P}_{4}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{3}
of order
(q+2)(s-2)+q
with co-conspirators
\mathcal{W}
(on level 2) such that the paths in
\mathcal{W}
are internally disjoint from
V(\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}C_{2}^{i})
.
This allows us to apply
Lemma

11.2
to
\mathcal{P}_{4}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{W}
, resulting in a transaction
\mathcal{P}_{4}
in
(G^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime})
of order
q
that is an
X^{\prime\prime}
-
Y^{\prime\prime}
-linkage.
Furthermore,
\bigcup\mathcal{P}_{4}
is disjoint from both
\bigcup\mathcal{W}
and the union of
\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}(H_{i}^{2}-V(C_{2}^{i}))
, where
H_{i}^{2}
is the outer graph of
C_{2}^{i}
in
\rho_{i}
.

Thus, since
\mathcal{P}_{4}
is a transaction of order
q=130kp+3p-80k+11
, we may now apply
Lemma

11.4
to find a transaction
\mathcal{P}_{5}
of order
p
, with co-conspirators
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
in
(G^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime})
(on level 3), that is disjoint from the union of
C_{3}^{i}
and the outer graphs
C_{3}^{i}
in
\rho_{i}
for all
i\in[k]
.
Since
V(\Omega^{\prime})=X^{\prime\prime}\cup Y^{\prime\prime}
, both
\mathcal{P}_{5}
and
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
can now be routed back onto
(G_{0},\Omega_{0})
whilst ensuring that the resulting transaction
\mathcal{P}_{6}
, for which there now exist co-conspirators
\mathcal{W}^{\prime\prime}
in
(G_{0},\Omega_{0})
(on level 3), is orthogonal to
\{C_{3}^{0},\ldots,C_{s}^{0}\}
.
Furthermore,
\mathcal{P}_{6}
is disjoint from the union of
C_{3}^{i}
and the outer graph of
C_{3}^{i}
in
\rho_{i}
for all
i\in[k]
.
This completes our proof.
∎

12
Transaction meshes

The following definition is inspired by the notion of “overflow” that was recently introduced by Gorsky, Kawarabayashi, Kreutzer, and Wiederrecht in
[
GKKW24
,
Gor24
]
in the context of a GMST-like result for directed graphs that exclude a large fractional packing of directed cycles of even length.
They are able to iteratively refine a society whilst keeping the number of apices under a fixed bound regardless of the number of iterations this process goes through.
We were not able to adapt this approach for our proof whilst keeping the bounds on the functions involved polynomial.
The idea of iteratively refining a decomposition with large strips of “flat” transactions to gradually embed more and more of the society in each iteration is nonetheless important to our approach.

Roughly speaking, a transaction mesh in a society
(G,\Omega)
represents a well-structured vortex-free rendition of a society
(H,\Omega)
with
H\subseteq G
in a disk with holes such that every vertex of
H
incident with an edge of
G
not in
H
is a node on the boundary of some hole. One can also think of a transaction mesh as a variant of a rendition in a disk, where the “vortices”
v_{i}
are not associated with distinct graphs
\sigma(v_{i})
, but rather with one graph which is attached to nodes in the sets
\tilde{v_{i}}
. Before we dive into the (quite technical) definition, let us briefly describe the infrastructure of a transaction mesh.

A transaction mesh parametrised with
\ell=0
is simply a cylindrical rendition
\rho
of
(G,\Omega)
around a vortex
c_{0}
with a large nest, and in such a transaction mesh, we denote by
(H_{1}^{0},\Omega_{1}^{0})
the society where
H_{1}^{0}
is the part of
G
drawn outside the vortex, and
\Omega_{1}^{0}=\Omega
. In general, a transaction mesh
has societies
(H_{1}^{\ell},\Omega_{1}^{\ell}),\ldots,(H_{\ell+1}^{\ell},\Omega_{\ell+1}^{\ell})
, and a collection of
\ell
transactions.
When the parameter increments from
\ell-1
to
\ell
, we add a transaction
\mathcal{P}_{\ell}
to the mesh. Roughly speaking, for some
j_{\ell}\in[\ell]
, the transaction is found in the society obtained from
(H_{j_{\ell}}^{\ell-1},\Omega_{j_{\ell}}^{\ell-1})
by adding the part of
G
which is outside the transaction mesh. The transaction
\mathcal{P}_{\ell}
is planar, exposed, and flat (after deleting a small set
A_{\ell}\subseteq V(G)
).
Upon adding the transaction to the mesh, we split the society
(H_{j_{\ell}}^{\ell-1},\Omega_{j_{\ell}}^{\ell-1})
into two societies
(H_{j_{\ell}}^{\ell},\Omega_{\ell}^{\ell})
and
(H_{\ell+1}^{\ell},\Omega_{\ell+1}^{\ell-1})
(more precisely, we find these societies with slightly smaller nests in the union of
(H_{j_{\ell}}^{\ell-1},\Omega_{j_{\ell}}^{\ell-1})
and the
\mathcal{P}_{j}
-strip society), see
figure

46
. The remaining societies
(H_{j}^{\ell},\Omega_{j}^{\ell})
with
j\in[\ell+1]\setminus\{j_{\ell},\ell+1\}
are obtained by slightly shrinking the societies
(H_{j}^{\ell-1},\Omega_{j}^{\ell-1})
.

One further notable feature of the definition is the treatment of the set
A_{\ell}
we mentioned earlier.
For technical reasons, we will distinguish between this set being empty – in which case we somehow got our flat transaction
\mathcal{P}_{\ell}
“for free” – and this set containing vertices.
In the second case, we demand that this set in fact is part of a cross that attaches “in the middle” of the strip of
\mathcal{P}_{\ell}
.
Of course, since
\mathcal{P}_{\ell}
is flat, this cross disappears as soon as we remove
A_{\ell}
.
The location of this cross allows us to protect it from the “vortices” of the transaction mesh.
This technicality is a herald of things to come, as
Definition

12.14
introduces three more types of objects we track in case
A_{\ell}
is empty.
Our reason for doing this is so that we can prove later on that, if we exclude the existence of a
K_{t}
-minor in
G
, the transaction mesh cannot grow for an unbounded number of iterations.

Definition 12.1

(Transaction mesh)
.

Let

Definition 12.2

(Districts)
.

Let
s,s^{\prime},a,p,\ell
be non-negative integers and let
(G,\Omega)
be a society, with an
(s,s^{\prime},a,p,\ell)
-transaction mesh
\mathfrak{M}=(H,\Omega,\mathcal{C},\mathfrak{P})
.
Further, let
\{(H_{i},\Omega_{i})\}_{i\in[\ell+1]}
be the areas of
\mathfrak{M}
, with the area-nests
\mathcal{C}_{i}
and apices
A_{i}
.

For all
i\in[\ell+1]
, we let
G_{i}
be the union of
H_{i}
and all
\bigcup\mathcal{C}_{i}
-bridges in
G-(\bigcup_{j=1}^{\ell}(V(\Omega_{j})\cup A_{j})\cup V(\Omega_{\ell+1}))
.
We call
(G_{i},\Omega_{i})
the
district corresponding to
(H_{i},\Omega_{i})
in
\mathfrak{M}
and call
\mathcal{C}_{i}
the
district-nest of
(G_{i},\Omega_{i})
.
We refer to the collection of all districts corresponding to areas of
\mathfrak{M}
as the
districts of
\mathfrak{M}
.
If it is clear that we are referring to a nest of an area or district, we do not further specify that it is an area- or district-nest.

Every district
(G_{i},\Omega_{i})
of
\mathfrak{M}
as above has a cylindrical rendition
\rho_{i}
around a unique vortex
c_{i}
such that the district-nest
\mathcal{C}_{i}
is a cozy nest of order
s-s^{\prime}\ell
around
c_{i}
in
\rho_{i}
.

One observation that is fundamental to this purpose of transaction meshes is that
(G,\Omega)
can now be described entirely via
H
and the districts of
\mathfrak{M}
.

Observation 12.3
.

Let
s,s^{\prime},a,p,\ell
be non-negative integers and let
(G,\Omega)
be a society, with an
(s,s^{\prime},a,p,\ell)
-transaction mesh
\mathfrak{M}=(H,\Omega,\mathcal{C},\mathfrak{P})
.
Further, let
\{(G_{i},\Omega_{i})\}_{i\in[\ell+1]}
be the districts of
\mathfrak{M}
.

We have
G=H\cup\bigcup_{i\in[\ell+1]}G_{i}
.

12.1
Padded transaction meshes

In some parts of our proof, we will need more infrastructure than a nest can provide.
We can reasonably expect this additional infrastructure to exist, since we use transactions to build the transaction mesh that are much larger than what we eventually require as the order of the district-nests.
For this purpose, we introduce the notion of a padding of a nest.

Definition 12.4

(Padded societies)
.

Let
s,p,\ell
be non-negative integers with
1\leq s\leq p
.
Let
(G,\Omega)
be a society with a cylindrical rendition
\rho
in a disk around a vortex
c_{0}
with a nest
\mathcal{C}
of order
s
.
Furthermore, let
\mathfrak{P}=\{\mathcal{P}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{P}_{\ell}\}
be a set of unexposed transactions in
(G,\Omega)
of order
p
in
\rho
.

Then we say that
(\mathcal{C},\mathfrak{P})
is an
(s,p,\ell)
-padding for
(G,\Omega)
, if
\mathcal{C}
and each
\mathcal{P}\in\mathfrak{P}
is cozy and for all
i\in[\ell]
, the transaction
\mathcal{P}_{i}=\{P_{1}^{i},\ldots,P_{p}^{i}\}
can be indexed naturally such that the
c_{0}
-disk of
P_{1}^{i}
in
\rho
is contained in the
c_{0}
-disk of
P_{j}^{i}
for all
j\in[2,p]
, and for all
j\in[s]
the path
P^{i}_{p+j-s}
intersects
C_{j}
.

Due to all parts of
(\mathcal{C},\mathfrak{P})
being grounded, the graph
\bigcup\mathcal{C}\cup\bigcup_{\mathcal{P}\in\mathfrak{P}}\mathcal{P}
contains a unique
c_{0}
-disk whose trace is described by a cycle
C
which we call the
innermost cycle of
(\mathcal{C},\mathfrak{P})
.
Note that any given society with a nest of order
s
has an
(s,q,0)
-padding for any positive integer
q
.

For our later task of building a transaction mesh, we require versions of two results which we have seen earlier.
First, we want to extend the notion of crooked witnesses to paddings and then present a version of
Lemma

10.5
.
The definition needed for this leans heavily on the idea that we can find nests in paddings that sensibly follow the structure of the infrastructure of the padding.

Remark 12.5
.

Let
s,p,\ell
be non-negative integers with
1\leq s\leq p
.
Let
(G,\Omega)
be a society with a cylindrical rendition
\rho
in a disk around a vortex
c_{0}
with an
(s,p,\ell)
-padding
(\mathcal{C}=\{C_{1},\ldots,C_{s}\},\mathfrak{P}=\{\mathcal{P}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{P}_{\ell}\})
.
Further, let
\mathcal{P}_{i}=\{P_{1}^{i},\ldots,P_{p}^{i}\}
be indexed naturally such that the
c_{0}
-disk of
P_{p}^{i}
contains the trace of all other paths in
\mathcal{P}_{i}
.

For each
i\in[s]
, we let
C_{i}^{\prime}
be the cycle whose trace bounds the closure of the
c_{0}
-disk in
C_{i}\cup\bigcup_{j\in[\ell]}P_{i}^{j}
.
Then
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=\{C_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,C_{s}^{\prime}\}
is a nest in
(G,\Omega)
.

We call
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
from the above remark the
inner nest
of
(\mathcal{C},\mathfrak{P})
, which in particular contains the innermost cycle of
(\mathcal{C},\mathfrak{P})
.
Note that
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
is unlikely to be cozy if
\ell\neq 0
and especially if
s<p
.
Though in the case in which
\ell=0
,
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
is simply
\mathcal{C}
itself.
There is however a society in which most of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
is cozy and we will work in this society later.
We therefore take a moment to prove this fact.

Lemma 12.6
.

Let
s,p,\ell
be non-negative integers with
2\leq s\leq p
.
Let
(G,\Omega)
be a society with a cylindrical rendition
\rho
in a disk around a vortex
c_{0}
with an
(s,p,\ell)
-padding
(\mathcal{C},\mathfrak{P})
, with
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=\{C_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,C_{s}^{\prime}\}
being its inner nest.
Further, let
(G^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime})
be the
C_{s}
-society in
\rho
and let
\rho^{\prime}
be the restriction of
\rho
to
G^{\prime}
.

Then
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\setminus\{C_{s}^{\prime}\}
is a cozy nest in
\rho^{\prime}
around
c_{0}
.

Proof.

Let
(\mathcal{C}=\{C_{1},\ldots,C_{s}\},\mathfrak{P}=\{\mathcal{P}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{P}_{\ell}\})
.
Further, let
\mathcal{P}_{i}=\{P_{1}^{i},\ldots,P_{p}^{i}\}
be indexed naturally such that the
c_{0}
-disk of
P_{p}^{i}
contains the trace of all other paths in
\mathcal{P}_{i}
.

Suppose that the outer graph of some
C_{i}^{\prime}
in
\rho^{\prime}
with
i\in[s-1]
contains a
C_{i}^{\prime}
-path
P
that sticks out away from
c_{0}
and does not intersect any cycle in
(\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\setminus\{C_{s}^{\prime}\})\setminus\{C_{i}^{\prime}\}
.
Then we know by definition that
C_{i}^{\prime}\subseteq C_{i}\cup\bigcup_{j\in[\ell]}P_{i}^{j}
and
C_{i+1}^{\prime}\subseteq C_{i+1}\cup\bigcup_{j\in[\ell]}P_{i+1}^{j}
, with the trace of either of these cycles bounding the
c_{0}
-disk of the subgraph of
G
they are respectively found within.
Due to this, we know that
P
does not intersect
C_{i+1}\cup\bigcup_{j\in[\ell]}P_{i+1}^{j}
.
We further note that each cycle and path in
(\mathcal{C},\mathfrak{P})
and
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
is grounded.
Thus
P
contains a
C
-path
P^{\prime}
that sticks out away from
c_{0}
for some
C\in\mathcal{C}
such that
P^{\prime}
is disjoint from all cycles in
\mathcal{C}\setminus\{C\}
, or a
Q
-path
P^{\prime\prime}
that sticks out away from
c_{0}
for some
P^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{P}\in\mathfrak{P}
such that
P^{\prime\prime}
is disjoint from all paths in
\mathcal{P}\setminus\{Q\}
.
Either option contradicts the coziness of the objects in
(\mathcal{C},\mathfrak{P})
and thus
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
must be cozy itself.
∎

Using the inner nest
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=\{C_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,C_{s}^{\prime}\}
of some padding, we can now say that a set
\mathcal{W}
of paths is a crooked witness for some transaction
\mathcal{P}
in
(G,\Omega)
on
C_{j}^{\prime}
for some
j\in[s]
.
This allows us to state our analogue of
Lemma

10.5
for this setting.
The proof of this lemma is entirely analogous to the proof of
Lemma

10.5
, so we omit it.

Lemma 12.7
.

Let
s,p,q,\ell
be non-negative integers with
2\leq s\leq p
.
Let
(G,\Omega)
be a society with a cylindrical rendition
\rho
in a disk around a vortex
c_{0}
with an
(s,p,\ell)
-padding
(\mathcal{C},\mathfrak{P})
, with the inner nest
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=\{C_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,C_{s}^{\prime}\}
, and let
\mathcal{P}
be a crooked transaction of order
8p-14+3q
in
(G,\Omega)
.

Then there exists an exposed transaction
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
of order
q
that has a crooked witness on
C_{2}^{\prime}
.

Moreover, said transaction and its crooked witness can be found in time
\mathbf{O}(q|E(G)|)
.

We mildly extend the notion of a radial linkage to a society
(G,\Omega)
with an
(s,p,\ell)
-padding
(\mathcal{C},\mathfrak{P})
, with
C
being its innermost cycle, by calling any
V(\Omega)
-
V(C)
-linkage
\mathcal{R}
a
radial linkage for
(\mathcal{C},\mathfrak{P})
(in
(G,\Omega)
)
.

The second lemma we need is yet another variant of
Lemma

10.6
.
Here we are more modest than in previous lemmas and only want to ensure that the transaction we find behaves in a very controlled way once it enters into the inner graph of
C_{1}
.
Note that here we do not orthogonalise the radial linkage we use to construct the linkage in this statement.

Lemma 12.8
.

Let
s,p,q,k
be non-negative integers with
2\leq s\leq p
.
Let
\{((G_{i},\Omega_{i}),\rho_{i},c_{i},\Delta_{i},\mathcal{C}_{i}=\{C_{1}^{i},\ldots,C_{s}^{i}\})\}_{i\in[0,k]}
be a full
(s,k)
-society configuration, such that for each
i\in[0,k]
the tuple
(\mathcal{C}_{i},\mathfrak{P}_{i})
is an
(s,p,\ell_{i})
-padding for
(G_{i},\Omega_{i})
with
\ell_{i}\leq\ell
and the inner nest
\mathcal{C}_{i}^{\prime}=\{C_{1}^{i},\ldots,C_{s}^{i}\}
.
Let
\mathcal{P}
be a transaction of order
(q+7)(p-2)+q
in
(G_{0},\Omega_{0})
with the end segments
X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}
, let
X
be the set of vertices in
V(C_{2}^{0})
that are seen first when traversing the paths in
\mathcal{P}
starting from
X^{\prime}
, and let
Y
be defined analogously for
Y^{\prime}
.
Further, let
\mathcal{W}
be a crooked witness for
\mathcal{P}
on
C_{2}^{0}
in
(G_{0},\Omega_{0})
.

Then there exists an
X
-
Y
-linkage
\mathcal{Q}
of order
q
in the union of
C_{2}^{0}
and the inner graph of
C_{2}^{0}
in
\rho_{i}
, such that
\bigcup\mathcal{Q}
is disjoint from both
\bigcup\mathcal{W}
and the union of
\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}(H_{i}^{2}-V(C_{2}^{i}))
, where
H_{i}^{2}
is the outer graph of
C_{2}^{i}
in
\rho_{i}
.

In particular,
\mathcal{Q}
can be found in time
\mathbf{O}(q|E(G)|)
.

Proof sketch.

Let
H_{i}=H_{i}^{2}-V(C_{2}^{i})
for each
i\in[0,k]
and let
G=G_{0}-\bigcup_{i=0}^{k}V(H_{i})
.
We apply
Menger’s theorem
within
G
to ask for an
X
-
Y
-linkage of order
p
.
Of course if this yields a linkage, we are immediately done and have justified the runtime bound we claim in our statement.

We may therefore instead assume that we find an
X
-
Y
-separator
S
of order at most
q-1
in
G
.
First let
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
be a transaction of order at least
(q+7)(p-2)+1
whose paths avoid
S
.
Further, let
Z=V(\bigcup\mathcal{W})\cap\bigcup_{i=0}^{k}V(C_{2}^{i})
be the set of endpoints the paths in
\mathcal{W}
have in
\bigcup_{i=0}^{k}V(C_{2}^{i})
.
We note that
|Z|\leq 8
, with the worst-case correspond to a doublecross.

Gangplanks and gangboards
for any
i\in[0,k]
and any
C_{2}^{i}
-path in
G_{0}
that sticks out away from
c_{i}
in
\rho_{i}
are then defined as in the proof sketch of
Lemma

11.2
.
Let
S^{\prime}=(S\cap\bigcup_{i=0}^{k}V(C_{2}^{i}))\cup Z
and note that
|S^{\prime}|\leq q+7
.
Suppose there exists some path
\widetilde{P}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}
such that whenever a gangplank of
\widetilde{P}
intersects
S^{\prime}
, the gangboard opposite to it is disjoint from
S^{\prime}
.
Then the union of
\widetilde{P}\cap G
and all of its gangplanks and gangboards that are disjoint from
S^{\prime}
forms a connected subgraph containing a vertex of
X
and a vertex of
Y
.
This contradicts the fact that
S
is an
X
-
Y
-separator and thus no such path
\widetilde{P}
may exist.

Clearly, no path in
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}
can be found in
G-S
and thus, every path in
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}
has a gangplank.
In particular, thanks to our prior observation, we conclude that every path in
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}
has a gangplank that is intersected by
S^{\prime}
and for which the gangboard opposing it is also intersected by
S^{\prime}
.
As we have
|\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}|\geq(q+7)(p-2)+1
and
|S^{\prime}|\leq q+7
, this means that there exists some
u\in S^{\prime}
that is contained in the gangplank of
p-1
paths
\widehat{P}_{1},\dots,\widehat{P}_{p-1}\in\widehat{\mathcal{P}}
, with all of these gangplanks being contained in
C_{2}^{i}
for some fixed
i\in[0,k]
.
From this point we reach a contradiction via entirely analogous methods to those presented in the proof of
Lemma

10.6
, using the fact that all transactions in the padding
(\mathcal{C}_{i},\mathfrak{P}_{i})
are cozy, and thus we omit this part.
∎

Before we start discussing padded societies in the context of transaction meshes, we discuss how to find a slightly less thick padding derived by moving into the
C_{s-k}
-society via the following lemma.

Lemma 12.9
.

Let
s,p,\ell,k
be non-negative integers with
1\leq k<s\leq p
.
Let
(G,\Omega)
be a society with an
(s,p,\ell)
-padding
(\mathcal{C}=\{C_{1},\ldots,C_{s}\},\mathfrak{P})
in a cylindrical rendition
\rho
in a disk
\Delta
around a vortex
c_{0}
. Then the
C_{s-k}
-society
(G^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime})
in
\rho
has an
(s-k-1,p-k-1,\ell)
-padding.

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{P}=\{\mathcal{P}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{P}_{\ell}\}
and
\mathcal{P}_{i}=\{P_{1}^{i},\ldots,P_{p}^{i}\}
for all
i\in[\ell]
.
To show that our lemma holds, we mainly need to show that we preserve the parameter
\ell
when moving into the
C_{s-k}
-society
(G^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime})
in
\rho
and that we can preserve the intersection properties of the cycles in
\mathcal{C}
and the outermost
s-k-1
paths in each transaction.

For this purpose it suffices to prove that for all
i\in[\ell]
and
j\in[p-k-1]
, there exists a unique
C_{s-k}
-path in
P_{j}^{i}
that sticks out towards
c_{0}
in
\rho
.
We show that this holds by induction over
k
, with
k=0
being trivial.
Suppose the statement does not hold and
P_{i}^{j}
does indeed have two distinct
C_{s-k}
-paths
P,P^{\prime}
that stick out towards
c_{0}
in
\rho
.
Consider the
P
-path and the
P^{\prime}
-path in
C_{s-k}
.
Due to the coziness of
\mathcal{P}_{i}
both of these paths must be intersected by
P_{j-1}^{i}
, which implies that
j<p
.
Let
u
be a vertex of
P_{j-1}^{i}
in the
P
-path in
C_{s-k}
and let
u^{\prime}
be a vertex of
P_{j-1}^{i}
in the
P^{\prime}
-path
C_{s-k}
.
We let
Q
be the
u
-
u^{\prime}
-path in
P_{j-1}^{i}
.
There must exist an
C_{s-k}
-path that sticks out away from
c_{0}
in
Q
.
For this not to contradict the coziness of
\mathcal{C}
,
Q
must intersect
C_{s-k-1}
, which implies that
k>0
.
But this implies that
P_{j-1}^{i}
has more than one
C_{s-k-1}
-path sticking out towards
c_{0}
, contradicting our induction hypothesis.
∎

The
(s-k-1,p-k-1,\ell)
-padding is called the
k
-narrowed padding derived from
(\mathcal{C},\mathfrak{P})
.

Next, we want to discuss how exactly we hope to find paddings in our areas and districts and what we expect of these objects.
For this sake, we introduce a notion that helps us orient ourselves inside of a transaction mesh.
If a transaction
\mathcal{P}_{i}
intersects an area, then at most half of that transaction intersects that area by construction.
We establish what half this is for each transaction.

Definition 12.10

(Perspective of an area or district)
.

Let
s,s^{\prime},a,p,\ell
be non-negative integers and let
(G,\Omega)
be a society, with an
(s,s^{\prime},a,p,\ell)
-transaction mesh
\mathfrak{M}=(H,\Omega,\mathcal{C},\mathfrak{P}=\{\mathcal{P}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{P}_{\ell}\})
.
Further, for all
i\in[\ell+1]
, let
(H_{i},\Omega_{i})
be the areas of
\mathfrak{M}
.

For each
i\in[\ell+1]
and
j\in[\ell]
, we let
h_{i,j}
be 0 if
E(H_{i})\cap E(\bigcup\mathcal{P}_{j})=\emptyset
.
Otherwise, we let
h_{i,j}=1
if
E(H_{i})\cap E(\bigcup P_{1}^{j})\neq\emptyset
and we let
h_{i,j}=p
if
E(H_{i})\cap E(P_{p}^{j})\neq\emptyset
.
By construction exactly one of these two options must hold if
E(H_{i})\cap E(\bigcup\mathcal{P}_{j})\neq\emptyset
, as we have
V(\Omega_{i})\subseteq V(\bigcup\mathcal{C}\cup\bigcup_{k=1}^{\ell}(Q_{L}^{k}\cup Q_{R}^{k}))
and
V(\Omega_{i})
is a separator in
H
, since
(H,\Omega)
has a vortex-free rendition in a disk.

We call
(h_{i,j})_{j\in[\ell]}
the
perspective of
(H_{i},\Omega_{i})
in
\mathfrak{M}
and call
(h_{i,j})_{i\in[\ell+1],j\in[\ell]}
the
perspectives in
\mathfrak{M}
.
The
perspective of a district in
\mathfrak{M}
is simply the perspective of the area in
\mathfrak{M}
that it corresponds to.

Using the notion of perspectives, we observe that each district in a transaction mesh with
\ell\geq 1
has a large radial linkage derived from the transaction with the highest index that intersects it.

Observation 12.11
.

Let
s,s^{\prime},a,p,q
be non-negative integers with
s\leq q\leq\lfloor\nicefrac{{p}}{{4}}\rfloor
, and let
\ell
be a positive integer.
Let
(G,\Omega)
be a society, with an
(s,s^{\prime},a,p,\ell)
-transaction mesh
\mathfrak{M}=(H,\Omega,\mathcal{C},\mathfrak{P}=\{\mathcal{P}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{P}_{\ell}\})
.
Further, we let
(H_{i},\Omega_{i})
be an area of
\mathfrak{M}
, let
\mathcal{C}_{i}
be the area-nest of order
s
for
(H_{i},\Omega_{i})
, and let
(h_{i,j})_{j\in[\ell]}
be the perspective of
(H_{i},\Omega_{i})
in
\mathfrak{M}
.

Let
j\in[\ell]
be the maximal such that
h_{i,j}\neq 0
, then there exists an radial linkage
\mathcal{R}_{i}
of order
q
for
\mathcal{C}_{i}
such that
\bigcup\mathcal{R}_{i}\subseteq\bigcup\{P_{1}^{j},\ldots,P_{q}^{j}\}
, if
h_{i,j}=1
, or
\bigcup\mathcal{R}_{i}\subseteq\bigcup\{P_{p+1-q}^{j},\ldots,P_{p}^{j}\}
, if
h_{i,j}=p
.

For
\ell\geq 1
, we call the radial linkage
\mathcal{R}_{i}
the
radial area-linkage of
(H_{i},\Omega_{i})
in
\mathfrak{M}
and transfer this object to the district corresponding to
(H_{i},\Omega_{i})
in
\mathfrak{M}
, where we call it the
radial district-linkage
.

In addition to this radial linkage, we also want to say that each district and area has their own padding.
In particular, we want to have something close to an
(s,\lfloor\nicefrac{{p}}{{2}}\rfloor,\ell_{i})
-padding for each district.
We therefore define a more restricted version of transaction meshes as follows.

Definition 12.12

(Padded transaction meshes)
.

Let
s,s^{\prime},a,p,\ell
be non-negative integers with
\max(a,s^{\prime})\ell\leq s
and
2s+2-(s^{\prime}\ell)\leq p
.
Let
\mathfrak{M}=(H,\Omega,\mathcal{C},\mathfrak{P}=\{\mathcal{P}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{P}_{\ell}\})
be an
(s,s^{\prime},a,p,\ell)
-transaction mesh in the society
(G,\Omega)
with the districts
\{(G_{i},\Omega_{i})\}_{i\in[\ell+1]}
each with a cylindrical rendition
\rho_{i}
around a vortex
c_{i}
with the district nest
\mathcal{C}_{i}
, and let
(h_{i,j})_{i\in[\ell+1],j\in[\ell]}
be the perspectives of
\mathfrak{M}
.
For all
i\in[\ell]
, let
q_{1}^{i}=\lfloor\nicefrac{{p}}{{2}}-s^{\prime}(\ell-i)\rfloor
, let
q_{p}^{i}=\lceil\nicefrac{{(p+1)}}{{2}}+s^{\prime}(\ell-i)\rceil
, and for all
i\in[\ell+1]
, let

I_{i}\coloneqq\{j\in[\ell]~\!\colon\!~V(P^{j}_{q_{h_{i,j}}})\cap V(\Omega_{i})\neq\emptyset\}.

We say that
\mathfrak{M}
is
padded
if for all
i\in[\ell+1]
, there exists an
(s-(s^{\prime}\ell+1),\lfloor\nicefrac{{p}}{{2}}-(s^{\prime}\ell+1)\rfloor,|I_{i}|)
-padding
(\mathcal{C}_{i},\mathfrak{P}_{i}=\{\mathcal{P}_{j}^{i}~\!\colon\!~j\in I_{i}\})
for
(G_{i},\Omega_{i})
such that
\mathcal{C}_{i}
is the outer nest of
(\mathcal{C}_{i},\mathfrak{P}_{i})
, and for all
v\in V(\Omega_{i})\cap V(\bigcup_{j=1}^{\ell}P^{j}_{q_{h_{i,j}}})
there exists a transaction
\mathcal{P}_{j}^{i}\in\mathfrak{P}_{i}
such that
v
lies outside of the
c_{0}
-disk of all paths
P\in\mathcal{P}_{j}^{i}
in
\rho_{i}
.

We note that it is possible to derive from
Definition

12.1
that every transaction mesh is in fact already padded by following the construction we have basically already laid out in the above definition.
Later on, we will prove this explicitly.
Note that
\mathfrak{P}_{i}
will be non-empty for all districts
(G_{i},\Omega_{i})
as along as
\ell\neq 0
due to the way in which we construct new areas in
M6
of
Definition

12.1
.

12.2
The tree-like structure of transaction meshes

For the proof of the core theorem of
Section

14
, we want to introduce a meta-structure that helps us analyse the development of a transaction mesh.
Note that here we do not need to care about paddings.
To facilitate this definition, we will have to recall some objects from
Definition

12.1
that we will use to inductively define the object we are interested in, but will not be explicitly accessed during our later proofs.
Our goal here is to establish four parameters that describe properties of a transaction mesh.
Over the course of
Section

14
, we will show that in any situation we encounter we can increase one of these parameters and establish that this will ultimately terminate in one of a selection of good outcomes for us.

Before we introduce the definition, we first want to discuss the role the contents of a particular set which we will call
\mathcal{Z}
will play.
The objects we choose here will seem odd to the reader, especially because there is no bound on their size despite the fact that we are deleting them.
This is due to the fact that we can easily separate these objects from the rest of the graph, because they are bounded by a separator of small size that is well-connected into the remainder of the graph.
To find these, we will later use the following extension of Menger’s theorem that is presented in
[
KTW21
]
as Lemma 6.8.
The runtime-estimate we add to their statement of the theorem is derived from their proof, which essentially consists of applying an algorithmic version of
Proposition

2.1

\mathbf{O}(k)
times.

Proposition 12.13

(Kawarabayashi, Thomas, and Wollan
[
KTW21
]
)
.

Let
k
be a non-negative integer, let
G
be a graph, and let
X,Y
be two non-empty subsets of
V(G)
.
Assume that there do not exist
k+1
vertex-disjoint
X
-
Y
-paths in
G
.
Then there exists a separation
(A,B)
of
G
with
X\subseteq A
and
Y\subseteq B
such that for every
v\in A\cap B
, there exists a path from
v
to
X
in
G[A]
and moreover, exactly one of the following holds:

i)

|A\cap B|=k
and there exists an
(A\cap B)
-
Y
-linkage of order
k
in
G[B]
, or

ii)

|A\cap B|<k
and
A\cap B\subseteq Y
.

Moreover, there exists an algorithm that, given
G
,
X
, and
Y
as input, finds a separation
(A,B)
corresponding to one of these two options in time
\mathbf{O}(k^{2}|E(G)|)
.

Our use of the set
\mathcal{Z}
here is inspired by a construction Kawarabayashi et al. use in steps 15 to 19 of their proof of Theorem 9.1 in
[
KTW21
]
.
Unless the reader wishes to read the entirety of
[
KTW21
]
, we do not recommend consulting this proof for a better understanding of our definition.

If
T
is a tree, we let
L(T)
be the set of its leaves and let
I(T)=V(T)\setminus L(T)
be its
internal vertices
.
Let
w
be designated as the
root
of
T
, then the
children
of a vertex
p\in V(T)
are the neighbours of
p
in
T
that are separated from
w
by
p
.
We also call
p
the
parent
of its children.
The
depth
of a vertex
v
in a tree
T
with the root
w
is the length of the unique
w
-
v
-path in
T
.
In particular, the root itself has depth 0 in
T
.

Let
S
be a non-empty set.
We say that a set
\mathcal{F}\subseteq 2^{S}

partitions

S
if the sets in
\mathcal{F}
are pairwise disjoint and non-empty, and
\bigcup_{F\in\mathcal{F}}F=S
.

Definition 12.14

(
\mathfrak{M}
-tree)
.

Let
s,s^{\prime},a,p,\ell,k
be non-negative integers with
\max(a,s^{\prime})\ell\leq s
and
2s+2\leq p
.
Let
\mathfrak{M}=(H,\Omega,\mathcal{C},\mathfrak{P})
be an
(s,s^{\prime},a,p,\ell)
-transaction mesh in the society
(G,\Omega)
with the areas
(H_{i},\Omega_{i})
and districts
(G_{i},\Omega_{i})
for
i\in[\ell+1]
.
For each
i\in[0,\ell]
, we denote the set of vortices in
\mathfrak{M}
as
V_{i}=\{v_{j}^{i}~\!\colon\!~j\in[i+1]\}

, set
V^{i}=\bigcup_{j=0}^{i}V_{j}
, and thus have
v_{1}^{0}\in V^{i}
for all
i\in[0,\ell]
.

We will define the contents of the tuple
\mathfrak{T}=(\mathfrak{M},T,\phi,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{F},\mathfrak{L})
, which we call the
\mathfrak{M}
-tree in
G^{\prime}
with
precision
k

Given two signatures
\bar{x}=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})

if we have

or

.
